is the randomized response mechanism probability,
\alpha_{0}
is the sub-test significance level,
\alpha
is the significance level, and
\varepsilon
is the privacy level.

Peña and Barrientos, 2022
show that SARRM is
(\varepsilon,0)
-DP with

\varepsilon=\log\!\big(\mathbb{P}(B_{1}>k)\big/\mathbb{P}(B_{0}>k)\big)

(24)

where
B_{0}\sim\mathrm{Binomial}(2k+1,1-p)
and
B_{1}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)+\mathrm{Binomial}(2k,1-p)
.

Peña and Barrientos, 2022
use the fact that the test statistic
T=\sum_{s=1}^{2k+1}r_{p}(t_{s})
defined in
Algorithm

3
follows a
\mathrm{Binomial}(2k+1,q_{p,\alpha_{0}})
distribution under the null, where
q_{p,\alpha_{0}}\coloneqq p\alpha_{0}+(1-p)(1-\alpha_{0})
, from which it can be deduced that SARRM has significance level
\alpha
provided that

\mathrm{level}(k,p,\alpha_{0})\coloneqq\sum_{\ell=k+1}^{2k+1}\binom{2k+1}{\ell}q_{p,\alpha_{0}}^{\ell}(1-q_{p,\alpha_{0}})^{2k+1-\ell}\leq\alpha.

(25)

Procedure 2

(
Peña and Barrientos, 2022
)
.

The aim is to determine the parameters
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
p\in(0.5,1)
and
\alpha_{0}\geq\alpha_{0,\mathrm{min}}
for
\alpha_{0,\mathrm{min}}=0.0025
(user-specified), such that SARRM achieves the given privacy level
\varepsilon
and significance level
\alpha
.
To start, set the value of
k
to 1.
The parameter
p
can then be chosen according to (
24
) to guarantee the
(\varepsilon,0)
-DP property.
If
\mathrm{level}(k,p,\alpha_{0,\mathrm{min}})\leq\alpha
, then there exists some
\alpha_{0}\geq\alpha_{0,\mathrm{min}}
such that
\mathrm{level}(k,p,\alpha_{0})=\alpha
which guarantees test level
\alpha
, all required conditions are then satisfied, and the values
k
,
p
and
\alpha_{0}
are used for SARRM.
If
\mathrm{level}(k,p,\alpha_{0,\mathrm{min}})>\alpha
, then level
\alpha
cannot be attained, the value
k+1
is then considered instead of
k
, and the procedure is repeated.
2
2

2

Following this procedure, we are able to replicate the results of
Peña and Barrientos, 2022
.
The procedure is guaranteed to terminate. If an upper bound on
k
is known (as in our experiments since the sample size is fixed), then the search of the parameter
k
can be performed using a bisection method which runs considerably faster.

Note that, in order to achieve a given privacy level
\varepsilon
and significance level
\alpha
, a certain number of subsets
2k+1
(determined by
2
) must be used. This is only possible if the sample size is greater than the number of subsets, that is, if
n\geq 2k+1
. If this is not the case, then SARRM simply cannot be run with that privacy level, significance level and sample size, which is a considerable limitation of SARRM.

D.3
TCS-ME: Trusted-Curator, perturbed Statistic, Mean Embedding

Raj et al., 2020
propose several variants of a DP kernel two-sample test:

•

considering both the trusted-curator and the no-trusted-entity privacy settings,

•

relying on the analytic Gaussian mechanism
(
Balle and Wang, 2018
)
to inject privacy noise either in the statistic or in the means and covariances of feature vectors,

•

using the two kernel statistics of
Jitkrittum et al., 2016
based on mean embeddings and on smooth characteristic functions,

•

either optimizing the test locations and bandwidth, or sampling locations and using the median heuristic bandwidth,

•

using the asymptotic
\chi^{2}
null distribution, or an approximation to the null distribution.

We compare against the most powerful of these tests (as can be seen in
Raj et al., 2020
), which they refer to as TCS-ME. This is the test based on the Trusted-Curator (TC) privacy setting (which is coherent with the privacy framework considered in for dpMMD), which directly perturbs the test Statistic (S) based on the kernel Mean Embeddings (ME), and which uses 20% of the data to optimize the test locations and kernel bandwidth for maximizing power, and which approximates the null.

TCS-ME is guaranteed to be
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP for
\delta>0
as it relies on the (analytic) Gaussian mechanism.
In our simulations, we set
\delta=10^{-5}
as in the experiments of
Raj et al., 2020
, and compare against
(\varepsilon,0)
-DP tests (a slightly more restrictive constraint).

As can be seen in
Section

C.3
, while TCS-ME controls the type I error at level
\alpha
in the one-dimensional case (which is the setting considered
Section

6.2
where we compared against TCS-ME), we observe that it can be extremely poorly calibrated (type I error up to
10\alpha
) when working in higher dimensions.

Appendix E
Proofs for the Main Text

This section collects the proofs of the results provided in the main text.

E.1
Proof of
Theorem

1

When
\mathcal{X}_{n}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
are exchangeable,
M_{0},\ldots,M_{B}
are exchangeable by construction. Moreover, since
M_{0},M_{1},\ldots,M_{B}
are distinct with probability one due to i.i.d. Laplace noises, the second result of
Lemma

15
proves the equality.

E.2
Proof of
Theorem

2

Let us denote the
1-\alpha
quantile of
\{M_{i}\}_{i=0}^{B}
and the
1-\alpha_{\star}
quantile of
\{M_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}
by
q_{1-\alpha}
and
r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}
, respectively, where

\displaystyle\alpha_{\star}=\max\bigg\{\biggl(\frac{B+1}{B}\alpha-\frac{1}{B}\biggr),\,0\bigg\}\in[0,1).

We then claim that

\displaystyle\mathds{1}(\widehat{p}_{\mathrm{dp}}\leq\alpha)\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{=}\mathds{1}(M_{0}>q_{1-\alpha})\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{=}\mathds{1}(M_{0}>r_{1-\alpha_{\star}})\mathds{1}\biggl(\alpha\geq\frac{1}{B+1}\biggr),

where identity (i) holds by the quantile representation of the permutation test in
Lemma

17
,
and identity (ii) holds by
Lemma

18
. Moreover,
Lemma

19
proves that
r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}
has the global sensitivity at most
\Delta_{T}
, and the triangle inequality yields that
T_{0}-r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}
has the global sensitivity at most
2\Delta_{T}
under condition (
4
).
Therefore the Laplace mechanism ensures that
M_{0}-r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}=T_{0}-r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP.
Finally, noting that the test function
\mathds{1}(\widehat{p}_{\mathrm{dp}}\leq\alpha)=\mathds{1}(M_{0}-r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}>0)\,\mathds{1}(\alpha\geq 1/(B+1))
is a function of
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP statistic, the post processing property of differential privacy (
Lemma

1
) proves that the given permutation test is differentially private at privacy levels
\varepsilon
and
\delta
. This completes the proof of
Theorem

1
.

E.3
Proof of
Theorem

3

The result of
Theorem

3
follows as a corollary of
Lemma

8
. In more detail, let
\mathcal{G}
be the sigma field generated by
\{\mathcal{X}_{n},\zeta_{0}\}
. Conditional on
\mathcal{G}
, observe that
\{M_{1},\ldots,M_{B}\}
are i.i.d. where the randomness arises from
\{\bm{\pi}_{i},\zeta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}
, and
M_{0}
is constant. Therefore, the proposed private test is pointwise consistent as the conditions in
Lemma

8
are satisfied.

E.4
Proof of
Theorem

4

By the quantile representation of the permutation test (
Lemma

17
), the type II error can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P}(\widehat{p}_{\mathrm{dp}}>\alpha)=\mathbb{P}_{P}\bigl(M_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}\bigr),

where
q_{1-\alpha,B}
is the
1-\alpha
quantile of
M_{0},M_{1},\ldots,M_{B}
. Let us split the proof into two steps:

•

In the first step, we present an upper bound for
q_{1-\alpha,B}
holding with high probability. In particular, the following holds

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha,B}\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+10\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Var}_{P}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]}{\alpha\beta}}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/4)

with high probability (say
1-3\beta/4
) under the condition that
B\geq 2\alpha^{-1}\max\{3\log(2\beta^{-1}),1-\alpha\}
. In fact, we can improve the condition for
B
at the cost of having a worse dependence on
\alpha
and
\beta
. Specifically, we prove at the end of this subsection that:

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha,B}\leq\mathbb{E}_{P,\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+8\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Var}_{P,\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]}{\gamma\alpha\beta}}+\frac{16}{\sqrt{\gamma\alpha\beta}}\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}

(26)

holds with probability at least
1-\beta/4
, provided that
B\geq\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha(1-\gamma)}
for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

•

In the second step, we use the above quantile bound to show that
control of the type II error is guaranteed under the condition in (
8
).

For simplicity, we omit the subscript
P
in the expectation, variance and probability operators throughout the proof.

Step 1 (Bounding the quantile).
We first focus on the quantile
q_{1-\alpha,B}
and present a high probability upper bound. To this end, we apply
Lemma

21
by setting
\mathcal{G}
therein to be the sigma field generated by
\mathcal{X}_{n}
. In particular, under the condition on
B
and by letting
q_{1-\alpha/6,\infty}
be the
1-\alpha/6
quantile of the conditional distribution of
T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta
given
\mathcal{X}_{n}
(or equivalently the limiting value of
q_{1-\alpha/6,B}
with
B=\infty
),
Lemma

21
along with
Remark

4
guarantees that the following event

\displaystyle E_{1}:=\big\{q_{1-\alpha,B}\leq q_{1-\alpha/6,\infty}\big\}

holds with probability at least
1-\beta/2
. We further define
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
and
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
as

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}\coloneqq\inf\Biggl\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{|\bm{\Pi}_{n}|}\sum_{\bm{\pi}\in\bm{\Pi}_{n}}\mathds{1}\bigl(T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\leq x\bigr)\geq 1-\alpha/12\Biggr\}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}\coloneqq 2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12),

where
|\bm{\Pi}_{n}|
denotes the cardinality of the set
\bm{\Pi}_{n}
. Then
Lemma

20
gives the inequality that
q_{1-\alpha/6,\infty}\leq q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}+q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
.

Next we further upper bound
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
and
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
by more manageable terms. To begin with
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
, Chebyshev’s inequality conditional on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\bigl\{T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\geq\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]+t\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\bigr\}\leq\frac{\mathrm{Var}_{\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]}{t^{2}}\quad\text{for any $t>0$.}

Consequently,
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
is bounded as

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}\leq\sqrt{\frac{12\mathrm{Var}_{\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]}{\alpha}}+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}].

Now define events
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
,

\displaystyle E_{2}:=\Big\{\mathrm{Var}_{\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]<8\beta^{-1}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathrm{Var}_{\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]\bigr)\Big\},

\displaystyle E_{3}:=\bigg\{\big|\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]-\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]\big|<\sqrt{8\beta^{-1}\mathrm{Var}\bigl(\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]\bigr)}\bigg\},

where each of the events holds with probability at least
1-\beta/8
by Markov’s inequality and Chebyshev’s inequality, respectively.
We emphasize that the expectation
\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]
is taken with respect to both
\mathcal{X}_{n}
and
\bm{\pi}
.
Under these events
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
, it can be seen that

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+\sqrt{\frac{96\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathrm{Var}_{\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]\bigr)}{\alpha\beta}}+\sqrt{\frac{8\mathrm{Var}\bigl(\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]\bigr)}{\beta}}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+10\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Var}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]}{\alpha\beta}}

where the second inequality uses the law of total variance. In summary, we have shown that with probability at least
1-3\beta/4
,

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha,B}\leq\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+10\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Var}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]}{\alpha\beta}}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12).

Step 2 (Bounding the type II error).
Given the upper bound for
q_{1-\alpha,B}
from the previous step, we now examine the type II error of the differentially private permutation test and show that it is bounded by
\beta
under the given condition. In particular, writing

\displaystyle R_{1}:=10\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Var}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]}{\alpha\beta}}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12),

\displaystyle R_{2}:=2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\beta/8),

the result from Step 1 yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(M_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B})~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(M_{0}\leq\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+R_{1}\bigr)+\frac{3\beta}{4}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{0}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}\leq\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+R_{1}\bigr)+\frac{3\beta}{4}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{0}\leq\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+R_{1}+R_{2}\bigr)+\frac{7\beta}{8},

(27)

where the last inequality holds since

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(-\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}>F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\beta/8)\bigr)\leq\frac{\beta}{8}.

We also remark that for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\beta\in(0,1-\alpha)
(implying that
\min\{\alpha,\beta\}<1/2
),

\displaystyle F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12)+F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\beta/8)\leq 7\max\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}.

(28)

In more detail, the quantile function of
\zeta
is given as

\displaystyle F_{\zeta}^{-1}(t)=\begin{cases}\log(2t)<0,\quad&\text{if $t<0.5$},\\
-\log\bigl(2(1-t)\bigr)\geq 0,\quad&\text{if $t\geq 0.5$.}\end{cases}

Based on this expression along with the fact that
\min\{\alpha,\beta\}<1/2
, we have

\displaystyle F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12)+F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\beta/8)~=~

\displaystyle\log(6/\alpha)+\log(4/\beta)=\log(24)+\log(1/\alpha)+\log(1/\beta)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 7\max\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}.

Hence, under the given condition of (
8
), we can ensure that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+R_{1}+R_{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n})]-\sqrt{8\beta^{-1}\mathrm{Var}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n})]}.

For clarity, we emphasize the notational difference between
\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n})]
and
\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]=\mathbb{E}_{P,\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]
where the former expectation is taken over
\mathcal{X}_{n}
, whereas the latter expectation is taken over both
\mathcal{X}_{n}
and
\bm{\pi}
. This bound together with Chebyshev’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{0}\leq\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+R_{1}+R_{2}\bigr)~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{0}\leq\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n})]-\sqrt{8\beta^{-1}\mathrm{Var}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n})]}\bigr)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{8\beta^{-1}\mathrm{Var}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n})]}\leq\mathbb{E}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n})]-T_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{8}.

Now combining the inequality (
27
) with the above yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(M_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B})\leq\beta.

This bound holds for any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
and the upper bound is independent of
P
. Hence the uniform guarantee stated in
Theorem

4
holds.

Proof of (
26
).
We now prove the inequality (
26
), which holds under a more relaxed condition for
B
. Observe that
q_{1-\alpha,B}
is the
1-\alpha
quantile of
\{M_{0},M_{1},\ldots,M_{B}\}
satisfying

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha,B}~=~

\displaystyle\inf\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{B+1}\sum_{i=0}^{B}\mathds{1}(M_{i}\leq x)\geq 1-\alpha\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\inf\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(M_{i}\leq x)\geq\frac{B+1}{B}(1-\alpha)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\inf\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(M_{i}\leq x)\geq 1-\gamma\alpha\bigg\}:=\tilde{q}_{1-\gamma\alpha,B},

where the last inequality holds under the condition that
B\geq\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha(1-\gamma)}
for
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Letting

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}_{B}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}M_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{B}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}(M_{i}-\widehat{\mu}_{B})^{2},

Chebyshev’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(M_{i}-\widehat{\mu}_{B}\geq t)\leq\frac{1}{t^{2}}\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{B}\overset{\mathrm{set}}{=}\gamma\alpha,

which implies that the quantile
\tilde{q}_{1-\gamma\alpha,B}
is bounded above by

\displaystyle\tilde{q}_{1-\gamma\alpha,B}\leq\widehat{\mu}_{B}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{B}}{\gamma\alpha}}.

(29)

Our strategy is to identify a high-probability upper bound for
\widehat{\mu}_{B}+\sqrt{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{B}/(\gamma\alpha)}
, which is again an upper bound for
q_{1-\alpha,B}
based on the previous observation. To this end, note that
M_{1},\ldots,M_{B}
are i.i.d. random variables conditional on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
. Hence, it holds by the law of total expectation that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{\mu}_{B}-\mathbb{E}[M_{1}])^{2}]~=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{\mu}_{B}-\mathbb{E}[M_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]+\mathbb{E}[M_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]-\mathbb{E}[M_{1}])^{2}]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{\mu}_{B}-\mathbb{E}[M_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}])^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}[M_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]-\mathbb{E}[M_{1}])^{2}]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(M_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n})]+\mathrm{Var}[\mathbb{E}[M_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n})]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}[M_{1}].

This together with Chebyshev’s inequality yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(|\widehat{\mu}_{B}-\mathbb{E}[M_{1}]|\geq t\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{t^{2}}\mathrm{Var}[M_{1}]\overset{\mathrm{set}}{=}\frac{\beta}{8}.

Moreover, by Markov’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\sigma}_{B}\geq t)\leq\frac{1}{t^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{B}]\leq\frac{1}{t^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(M_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n})]\overset{\mathrm{set}}{=}\frac{\beta}{8}.

Combining the above two inequalities along with (
29
) yields that

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha,B}\leq\mathbb{E}[M_{1}]+\sqrt{\frac{8}{\beta}\mathrm{Var}[M_{1}]}+\sqrt{\frac{8}{\gamma\alpha\beta}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(M_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n})]}

holding with probability at least
1-\beta/4
. Since
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(M_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n})]\leq\mathrm{Var}[M_{1}]
and
M_{1}
has the same distribution as
T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta
, we further have

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha,B}\leq\mathbb{E}_{P,\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]+8\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Var}_{P,\bm{\pi}}[T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})]}{\gamma\alpha\beta}}+\frac{16}{\sqrt{\gamma\alpha\beta}}\frac{\Delta_{T}}{\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}

with probability at least
1-\beta/4
, provided that
B\geq\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha(1-\gamma)}
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
. This proves the inequality (
26
).

E.5
Proof of
Lemma

5

We start with the proof of the upper bound, followed by the proof of the lower bound.

E.5.1
Upper Bound

To simplify the proof, we may exploit the permutation invariance property of
\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})
within
\{X_{\pi_{1}},\ldots,X_{\pi_{n}}\}
and
\{Y_{\pi_{n+1}},\ldots,Y_{\pi_{n+m}}\}
, and focus on two specific cases of neighboring dataset:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi},a}=\{X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{\pi_{n}},Y_{\pi_{n+1}},\ldots,Y_{\pi_{n+m}}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi},b}=\{X_{\pi_{1}},\ldots,X_{\pi_{n}},Y_{\pi_{n+1}}^{\prime},\ldots,Y_{\pi_{n+m}}\},

where
X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime}
is an i.i.d. copy of
X_{\pi_{1}}
and
Y_{\pi_{n+1}}^{\prime}
is an i.i.d. copy of
Y_{\pi_{n+1}}
. The bound for the other neighboring datasets can be proven by following the same lines of the proof.

Starting with the neighboring dataset
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi},a}
, note that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{\pi_{i}})-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(Y_{\pi_{n+j}})\biggr\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{\pi_{i}})-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(Y_{\pi_{n+j}})+\frac{1}{n}f(X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime})-\frac{1}{n}f(X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime})\biggr\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\Bigl(f(X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime})+\sum_{i=2}^{n}f(X_{\pi_{i}})\Bigr)-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(Y_{\pi_{n+j}})+\frac{1}{n}f(X_{\pi_{1}})-\frac{1}{n}f(X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime})\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\Bigl(f(X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime})+\sum_{i=2}^{n}f(X_{\pi_{i}})\Bigr)-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(Y_{\pi_{n+j}})\biggr\}+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}f(X_{\pi_{1}})-\frac{1}{n}f(X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime})\biggr\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi},a})+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}f(X_{\pi_{1}})-\frac{1}{n}f(X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime})\biggr\},

where the inequality uses the triangle inequality. Since
|f(x)-f(y)|=|\langle f,k(x,\cdot)-k(y,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}|\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\sqrt{k(x,x)+k(y,y)-2k(x,y)}
by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality as well as the reproducing kernel property, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}f(X_{\pi_{1}})-\frac{1}{n}f(X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime})\biggr\}\leq\frac{1}{n}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}|f(X_{\pi_{1}})-f(X_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2K}}{n},

where the second inequality uses the fact that
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq 1
and
0\leq k(x,y)\leq K
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{S}
. We therefore observe
\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})-\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi},a})\leq\sqrt{2K}/n
. Since the same argument holds with the roles of
\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi},a}
reversed, we conclude that

\displaystyle\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})-\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi},a})\bigr|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2K}}{n}.

Next, for the dataset
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi},b}
, we can use essentially the same argument to show that

\displaystyle\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})-\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi},b})\bigr|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2K}}{m}.

Since
n\leq m
, the sensitivity is bounded by
\sqrt{2K}/n
for any neighboring datasets and the upper bound is independent of
\bm{\pi}
. Therefore it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{\pi}\in\bm{\Pi}_{n+m}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{X}_{n+m},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m}:\\
d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m})\leq 1\end{subarray}}\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})-\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})\bigr|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2K}}{n},

which in turn proves the first claim in
Lemma

5
.

E.5.2
Lower Bound

We now further assume that the kernel
k
is translation invariant, and has non-empty level sets in
\mathbb{S}
. In this case, we show that the inequality becomes an equality by considering appropriate sets
\mathcal{X}_{n+m}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m}
. More specifically, for a translation invariant kernel
k
and a constant
\epsilon>0
, consider values
x_{a}
and
x_{b}
in
\mathbb{S}
such that
k(x_{a},x_{b})=\epsilon_{\star}\leq\epsilon
, which is guaranteed by our assumption that
k
has non-empty level sets. Let
Y_{1}=\ldots=Y_{n}=Z_{1}=\ldots=Z_{m}=x_{a}
. In this scenario, the empirical MMD becomes zero for any permutation
\bm{\pi}\in\bm{\Pi}_{n+m}
. Let
Y_{1}^{\prime}=x_{b}
so that
k(Y_{1},Y_{1}^{\prime})=\epsilon_{\star}
. Then based on the closed form expression of the empirical MMD (
10
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}^{2}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})~=~

\displaystyle\frac{(n-1)^{2}+1}{n^{2}}K+\frac{2(n-1)}{n^{2}}\epsilon_{\star}+K-\frac{2m(n-1)}{nm}K-\frac{2m}{nm}\epsilon_{\star}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{2K}{n^{2}}-\frac{2\epsilon_{\star}}{n^{2}}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{\pi}\in\bm{\Pi}_{n+m}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{X}_{n+m},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m}:\\
d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m})\leq 1\end{subarray}}\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})-\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})\bigr|\geq\frac{\sqrt{2(K-\epsilon_{\star})}}{n}.

Since
\epsilon
is an arbitrary positive number and
\epsilon_{\star}\leq\epsilon
, we may take
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
and thus the lower bound becomes
\sqrt{2K}/n
, indicating that the upper bound
\sqrt{2K}/n
is tight under the given conditions. This proves the second claim in
Lemma

5
.

E.6
Proof of
Theorem

5

Let us prove the three claims made in
Theorem

5
below.

•

Proof of Differential Privacy.
This result follows by
Theorem

2
along with
Lemma

5
.

•

Proof of Validity.
This result follows by
Theorem

1
.

•

Proof of Consistency.
Having
Lemma

8
in place, the only condition we need to verify for consistency is
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n})=0
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be the sigma field generated by
\{\zeta_{0},\mathcal{X}_{n+m}\}
, and let

\displaystyle W_{i,n}=M_{i}=\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}_{i}})+\frac{2\sqrt{2K}}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\zeta_{i},\quad\text{for each $i\in\{0,1\}$,}

where we recall
\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}=\varepsilon+\log(1/(1-\delta))
.
Our goal is to show that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(M_{0}\leq M_{1})=0
under the conditions. Since the pair of distributions
(P,Q)
and kernel
k
do not vary with
n
, we consider the kernel bound
K
to be a fixed constant throughout. Since
K
is a constant and
(n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{-1}\rightarrow 0
, the scale of the Laplace noise
\frac{2\sqrt{2K}}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}
goes to zero as well. Thus Slutsky’s theorem yields
\frac{2\sqrt{2K}}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\times\zeta_{i}=o_{P}(1)
for each
i\in\{0,1\}
. Next, for
i=0
,
Gretton et al., 2012
indicate that the unpermuted MMD statistic satisfies
\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}_{0}})\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)
where the symbol
\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}
denotes convergence in probability. Hence another application of Slutsky’s theorem yields
M_{0}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)
. On the other hand, we have
\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}_{1}})\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}0
by
Lemma

11
, and again Slutsky’s theorem gives
M_{1}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}0
. Combining these two results, we further have
M_{0}-M_{1}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)
. To complete the proof, note that
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)\eqqcolon\epsilon>0
is assumed to be a fixed positive constant and therefore

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(M_{0}\leq M_{1})\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(|M_{0}-M_{1}|\geq\epsilon)=0,

by the definition of convergence in probability. This completes the proof of
Theorem

5
.

E.7
Proof of
Lemma

6

We prove the upper bound result and the lower bound result in order.

E.7.1
Upper Bound

We begin by considering the upper bound result of
Lemma

6
. Let us consider the dataset
\mathcal{X}_{n}=\{(Y_{1},Z_{1}),\ldots,(Y_{n},Z_{n})\}
and its neighboring dataset denoted by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}=\{(Y_{1}^{\prime},Z_{1}^{\prime}),(Y_{2},Z_{2}),\ldots,(Y_{n},Z_{n})\}\coloneqq\{(\tilde{Y}_{1},\tilde{Z}_{1}),(\tilde{Y}_{2},\tilde{Z}_{2}),\ldots,(\tilde{Y}_{n},\tilde{Z}_{n})\}

where
(Y_{1}^{\prime},Z_{1}^{\prime})
is an i.i.d. copy of
(X_{1},Y_{1})
independent of everything else. That is,
\mathcal{X}_{n}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
differ in their first component. We only prove the result of
Lemma

6
focusing on
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
, and the proof for the other neighboring datasets can be derived similarly due to the symmetric structure of the empirical HSIC.

For a given permutation
\bm{\pi}
, denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}}
the neighboring dataset
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
whose
Z
values are permuted based on
\bm{\pi}
. Let us divide the cases into two: (i)
\pi_{1}=1
and (ii)
\pi_{1}\neq 1
, and provide the proofs separately. For the first case where
\pi_{1}=1
, we write
k(y,\cdot)=\psi_{Y}(y)
and
\ell(z,\cdot)=\psi_{Z}(z)
. We also write the sample mean of
\psi_{Y}(Y_{1}),\ldots,\psi_{Y}(Y_{n})
as
\overline{\psi}_{Y}
and the sample mean of
\psi_{Z}(Z_{1}),\ldots,\psi_{Z}(Z_{n})
as
\overline{\psi}_{Z}
. Similarly write the sample mean of
\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1}),\ldots,\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{n})
as
\tilde{\psi}_{Y}
and the sample mean of
\psi_{Z}(\tilde{Z}_{1}),\ldots,\psi_{Z}(\tilde{Z}_{n})
as
\tilde{\psi}_{Z}
. Then by adding and subtracting the same terms, we can connect
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})
with
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})
as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{\pi_{i}})-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{\pi_{j}})\Biggr\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(Y_{i})-\overline{\psi}_{Y}\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{i}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(Y_{i})-\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{i})+\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{i})-\overline{\psi}_{Y}+\tilde{\psi}_{Y}-\tilde{\psi}_{Y}\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{i}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{i})_{a}}{=}~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(Y_{i})-\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{i})+\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{i})-\tilde{\psi}_{Y}\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{i}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(Y_{1})-\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1})\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{1}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{i})-\tilde{\psi}_{Y}\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{i}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(Y_{1})-\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1})\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{1}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}

\displaystyle+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{i})-\tilde{\psi}_{Y}\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{i}})-\psi_{Z}(\tilde{Z}_{\pi_{i}})+\psi_{Z}(\tilde{Z}_{\pi_{i}})-\tilde{\psi}_{Z}+\tilde{\psi}_{Z}-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{ii})_{a}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(Y_{1})-\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1})\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{1}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1})-\tilde{\psi}_{Y}\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{1}})-\psi_{Z}(\tilde{Z}_{\pi_{1}})\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}},

where step
(\mathrm{i})_{a}
uses
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\overline{\psi}_{Y}-\tilde{\psi}_{Y})\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{i}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\}=0
, and step
(\mathrm{ii})_{a}
follows similarly. Therefore

\displaystyle\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\bigr|~\leq~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg|\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(Y_{1})-\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1})\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{1}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg|\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1})-\tilde{\psi}_{Y}\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{1}})-\psi_{Z}(\tilde{Z}_{\pi_{1}})\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg|

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathrm{(I)}+\mathrm{(II)}.

For the term (I), the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality along with the fact
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}\leq 1
yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{(I)}~\leq~

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{1}{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(Y_{1})-\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1})\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{1}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\bigg\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\big\|\psi_{Y}(Y_{1})-\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1})\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\big\|\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{1}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}_{b}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\|\psi_{Y}(Y_{1})-\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1})\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\big\|\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{1}})-\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{i}})\big\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}_{b}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\frac{n-1}{n^{2}}\sqrt{2K}\sqrt{2L}.

In the above, step
(\mathrm{i})_{b}
uses Jensen’s inequality, and step
(\mathrm{ii})_{b}
follows since

\displaystyle\|\psi_{Y}(y)-\psi_{Y}(y^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sqrt{k(y,y)+k(y^{\prime},y^{\prime})-2k(y,y^{\prime})}\leq\sqrt{2K}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\|\psi_{Z}(z)-\psi_{Z}(z^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}=\sqrt{\ell(z,z)+\ell(z^{\prime},z^{\prime})-2\ell(z,z^{\prime})}\leq\sqrt{2L},

for any
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Y}
and
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
. The second term (II) can be similarly handled and shown to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{(II)}~\leq~\frac{n-1}{n^{2}}\sqrt{2K}\sqrt{2L}.

Thus it holds that

\displaystyle\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\bigr|\leq\frac{4(n-1)\sqrt{KL}}{n^{2}}.

The other case where
\pi_{1}\neq 1
can be proven similarly. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\pi_{1}=2
. Then by following the same calculations as before, we have

\displaystyle\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\bigr|~\leq~

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg|\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(Y_{1})-\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{1})\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{1}})-\overline{\psi}_{Z}\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg|\Bigg\langle f,\,\frac{1}{n}\big\{\psi_{Y}(\tilde{Y}_{2})-\tilde{\psi}_{Y}\big\}\big\{\psi_{Z}(Z_{\pi_{2}})-\psi_{Z}(\tilde{Z}_{\pi_{2}})\big\}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{4(n-1)\sqrt{KL}}{n^{2}},

which completes the proof of the first claim of
Lemma

6
.

E.7.2
Lower Bound

We now construct an example where the sensitivity becomes
4(n-2.5)n^{-2}\sqrt{KL}
. For a given
\epsilon\in(0,\min\{K,L\})
, we may assume that there exist
y_{a},y_{b}\in\mathbb{Y}
and
z_{a},z_{b}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
k(y_{a},y_{b})=\epsilon_{\star}\leq\epsilon
and
\ell(z_{a},z_{b})=\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}\leq\epsilon
, since
k
and
\ell
are assumed to have non-empty level sets on
\mathbb{Y}
and
\mathbb{Z}
. Consider the two datasets

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix},

where
d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n})=1
. We then consider a permutation
\bm{\pi}=(2,1,3,4,\ldots,n)
and denote the corresponding permuted datasets as

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{b}\\
y_{b}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix}.

Using the closed form expression of the squared HSIC in (
12
) and the property of translation invariant kernels, in particular
k(y,y)=K
and
\ell(z,z)=L
for all
y,z
, it can be seen that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})~=~

\displaystyle\frac{4+(n-2)^{2}}{n^{2}}KL+\frac{4(n-2)\epsilon_{\star}\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\bigg\{\frac{4+(n-2)^{2}}{n^{2}}K+\frac{4(n-2)}{n^{2}}\epsilon_{\star}\bigg\}\bigg\{\frac{4+(n-2)^{2}}{n^{2}}L+\frac{4(n-2)}{n^{2}}\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}\bigg\}

\displaystyle-~4

\displaystyle\bigg\{\frac{4}{n^{3}}KL+\frac{2K\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}(n-2)}{n^{3}}+\frac{2L\epsilon_{\star}(n-2)}{n^{3}}+\frac{(n-2)^{2}\epsilon_{\star}\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}}{n^{3}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle-~2

\displaystyle(n-2)\bigg\{\frac{4\epsilon_{\star}\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}}{n^{3}}+\frac{2\epsilon_{\star}L(n-2)}{n^{3}}+\frac{2K\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}(n-2)}{n^{3}}+\frac{KL(n-2)^{2}}{n^{3}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{16(n-2)^{2}}{n^{4}}KL+C_{1}\epsilon_{\star}\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}+C_{2}\epsilon_{\star}+C_{3}\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime},

where
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
are constants that only depend on
K,L,n
. A similar calculation shows

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})~=~

\displaystyle\frac{4}{n^{4}}KL+C_{1}^{\prime}\epsilon_{\star}\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}+C_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon_{\star}+C_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime},

where
C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}
are constants that only depend on
K,L,n
. These results together with the reverse triangle inequality yields

\displaystyle\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\bigr|~\geq~

\displaystyle\bigg|\sqrt{\frac{16(n-2)^{2}}{n^{4}}KL}-\sqrt{\frac{4}{n^{4}}KL}\bigg|-h(\epsilon_{\star},\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime},K,L,n)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{4(n-2.5)}{n^{2}}\sqrt{KL}-h(\epsilon_{\star},\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime},K,L,n),

where
h(\epsilon_{\star},\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime},K,L,n)
is some function of
\epsilon_{\star},\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime},K,L,n
, which goes to zero as
\epsilon_{\star}\rightarrow 0
and
\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
for each fixed
K,L,n
. Therefore it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{\pi}\in\bm{\Pi}_{n}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}:\\
d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n})\leq 1\end{subarray}}\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\bigr|\geq\frac{4(n-2.5)}{n^{2}}\sqrt{KL}-h(\epsilon_{\star},\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime},K,L,n),

and the lower bound result follows by letting
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
given that
\max\{\epsilon_{\star},\epsilon_{\star}^{\prime}\}\leq\epsilon
. This completes the proof of
Lemma

6
.

E.8
Proof
Theorem

6

Let us prove the three claims made in
Theorem

6
below.

•

Proof of Differential Privacy.
This result follows by
Theorem

2
along with
Lemma

6
.

•

Proof of Validity.
This result follows by
Theorem

1
.

•

Proof of Consistency.
The proof of consistency for
\phi_{\mathrm{dpHSIC}}
is essentially the same as that for
\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}
in
Theorem

5
. We only need to verify that
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}_{0}})\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ})
and
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}_{1}})\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}0
. Indeed, the first claim follows by
Lemma

14
and the second claim follows by
Lemma

12
, given that
\alpha,K,L
are fixed quantities. The remaining steps are the same as those for the proof of
Theorem

5
and thus we omit the details.

E.9
Proof of
Theorem

7

The proof of
Theorem

7
follows the same structure as that of
Theorem

4
. The main difference is that we make use of exponential concentration inequalities of the MMD statistic in order to obtain the logarithmic dependence on both
\alpha
and
\beta
. Throughout the proof, we denote positive constants that only depend on
K
and
\tau
by
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
whose values may vary in different places. As in the proof of
Theorem

4
, we build on the quantile representation of the permutation test (
Lemma

17
) from which we see that the type II error can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[1-\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}]=\mathbb{P}(M_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}),

where
q_{1-\alpha,B}
denotes the
1-\alpha
quantile of
\{M_{0},M_{1},\ldots,M_{B}\}
, and
M_{i}
is given as
\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}_{i}})+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{i}
for
i\in\{0\}\cup[B]
.

Step 1 (Bounding the quantile).
As in the proof of
Theorem

4
, denote by
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
and
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
, the
1-\alpha/12
quantile of the conditional distribution of
\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})
given
\mathcal{X}_{n+m}
and that of
2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta
, respectively. Then following the argument given in
Section

E.4
and under the condition for
B
, we see that the inequality
q_{1-\alpha,B}\leq q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}+q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
holds with probability at least
1-\beta/2
.

Next we further upper bound
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
and
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
by more manageable terms. Applying
Lemma

10
with the condition
n\leq m\leq\tau n
yields the following inequality:

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\log\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr)}.

On the other hand, as noted in
Section

E.4
,
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
is simply the
1-\alpha/12
quantile of
2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta
, namely

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}=2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12).

Therefore, the following inequality holds with probability at least
1-\beta/2
:

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha,B}\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\log\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr)}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12).

(30)

Remark 3

(Condition for the sample-size ratio)
.

The condition
n\leq m\leq\tau n
is required to apply
Lemma

10
under which we can obtain the logarithmic factor of
\alpha
in the upper bound for
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
. This condition can be eliminated by using
Lemma

11
, which shows that

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}\leq C_{2}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n\alpha}},

without the condition for the sample-size ratio constraint. It remains an open question whether we can achieve the logarithmic factor of
\alpha
in the upper bound without the constraint
n\leq m\leq\tau n
, which we leave for future research.

Step 2 (Bounding the type II error).
Let
(P,Q)
be a pair of distributions in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}\!(\rho)
. Given
(P,Q)
,
Lemma

13
(concentration inequality for
\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}
) ensures that the following event
E_{1}
:

\displaystyle E_{1}\coloneqq\biggl\{\bigg|\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)-\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m})\bigg|\leq C_{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(8/\beta)}{n}}\biggr\}

holds with probability at least
1-\beta/4
. Notice that the inverse cumulative distribution function of
\zeta\sim\mathsf{Laplace}(0,1)
is given by

\displaystyle F^{-1}_{\zeta}(p)\coloneqq-\mathrm{sign}(p-0.5)\times\log(1-2|p-0.5|)\quad\text{for $p\in(0,1)$.}

This yields that the probability of the event

\displaystyle E_{2}\coloneqq\big\{2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}>2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F^{-1}_{\zeta}(\beta/4)\big\}

is equal to
1-\beta/4
.
Using these preliminary results, it can be seen that the type II error of
\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[1-\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}]~\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{=}~\mathbb{P}(M_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B})~\overset{(\mathrm{ii})}{=}~\mathbb{P}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m})+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}\bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{iii})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F^{-1}_{\zeta}(\beta/4)\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}+C_{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(8/\beta)}{n}}\biggr)+\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{c})+\mathbb{P}(E_{2}^{c})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(iv)}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}+C_{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(8/\beta)}{n}}-2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F^{-1}_{\zeta}(\beta/4)\biggr)+\frac{\beta}{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{v})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(12/\alpha)}{n}}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+C_{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(8/\beta)}{n}}-2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F^{-1}_{\zeta}(\beta/4)\biggr)+\beta,

where step (i) uses
Lemma

17
, step (ii) uses the definition of
M_{0}
, step (iii) follows by the union bound, step (iv) uses the properties of the events
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, and step (v) uses the inequality given in (
30
) and the union bound.

Since
\beta/4<1/2
and
1-\alpha/12>1/2
, it follows that

\displaystyle F^{-1}_{\zeta}(\beta/4)=\log\biggl(\frac{\beta}{2}\biggr)\quad\text{and}\quad F^{-1}_{\zeta}(1-\alpha/12)=\log\biggl(\frac{6}{\alpha}\biggr),

which gives

\displaystyle 2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12)-2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F^{-1}_{\zeta}(\beta/4)~=~

\displaystyle 2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\biggl\{\log\biggl(\frac{6}{\alpha}\biggr)+\log\biggl(\frac{2}{\beta}\biggr)\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C_{4}\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\max\biggl\{\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\beta}\biggr)\biggr\}.

Consequently, the type II error of the
\mathrm{dpMMD}
test is bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[1-\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)\leq C_{5}\sqrt{\frac{\max\bigl\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}}{n}}+C_{6}\frac{\max\bigl\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\biggr)+\beta

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\beta,

where the last inequality holds by taking
C_{K,\tau}
to be larger than, for instance,
2\max\{C_{5},C_{6}\}+1
in the theorem statement. Finally, we note that the above upper bound is independent of
(P,Q)
, and thus complete the proof by taking the supremum on both sides over
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}\!(\rho)
.

E.10
Proof of
Theorem

8

Under the conditions of
Theorem

8
, we analyze that the minimax separation
\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{MMD}}(\alpha,\beta,\varepsilon,\delta,m,n)=\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{MMD}}
in the non-privacy regime and in the privacy regime, separately. In both regimes, we use a common trick that reduces the two-sample problem to the one-sample problem (also known as goodness-of-fit testing) stated below.

Reducing the two-sample problem to the one-sample problem.

We can think of the one-sample problem as a special case of the two-sample problem by assuming that we have access to an infinite number of observations from one of the two distributions (say
Q
). That is, we know the entire information of the distribution
Q
and
m=\infty
.
Arias-Castro et al., 2018
builds on this simple observation and formalizes that the minimax risk of the two-sample problem is greater than or equal to that of the one-sample problem. We follow this strategy and work with the one-sample problem.

More formally, pick one distribution
Q_{0}
(specified later in the proof) from
\mathcal{P}
and fix it throughout. Let
\phi:\mathcal{X}_{n}\mapsto\{0,1\}
be a test function and define the set of
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP level
\alpha
one-sample tests as

\displaystyle\Phi_{\alpha,\varepsilon,\delta,Q_{0}}\coloneqq\Big\{\phi:\mathbb{E}_{Q_{0}}[\phi]\leq\alpha\ \text{and}\ \text{$\phi$ is $(\varepsilon,\delta)$-DP}\Big\}.

Then, letting
\mathcal{P}_{1,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}(\rho;Q_{0})\coloneqq\{P\in\mathcal{P}:\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q_{0})\geq\rho\}
,
Arias-Castro et al., 2018
yields

\displaystyle\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{MMD}}\geq\inf\Bigl\{\rho>0:\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\varepsilon,\delta,Q_{0}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}(\rho;Q_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P}[1-\phi]\leq\beta\Big\}.

Therefore, once we prove

\displaystyle\inf\Bigl\{\delta>0:\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\varepsilon,Q_{0}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}(\rho;Q_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P}[1-\phi]\leq\beta\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle C_{\eta}\max\Biggl\{\min\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\bigg\},\,\min\biggl\{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n(\varepsilon+\delta)},\,1\biggr\}\Biggr\}

(31a)

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle C_{\eta}\max\Biggl\{\min\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\bigg\},\,\min\biggl\{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n\big(\varepsilon+\log(1/(1-\delta))\big)},\,1\biggr\}\Biggr\},

(31b)

for some
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
, then the desired result of
Theorem

8
follows.
The two lower bounds in (
31b
) are proved in
Sections

E.10.1
and
E.10.2
.
The inequality (
31a
)
\geq
(
31b
) holds as
\log(1/(1-\delta))\geq\delta
for all
\delta\in[0,1)
, and is sufficient to prove
Theorem

8
.
Nonetheless, to provide intuition on the tightness of the lower bound, it is interesting to understand that when
\alpha\asymp\beta
the two rates in
Equations

31a
and
31b
are the same, which we prove in
Section

E.10.3
.

E.10.1
Non-privacy regime

We first show that
\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{MMD}}\geq C_{\eta}\min\big\{\sqrt{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))/n},1\big\}
in the non-privacy regime. Writing the set of non-private level
\alpha
tests (
i.e.
,
\varepsilon\to\infty
) as

\displaystyle\Phi_{\alpha,\infty,Q_{0}}\coloneqq\Big\{\phi:\mathbb{E}_{Q_{0}}[\phi]\leq\alpha\Big\},

we clearly have
\Phi_{\alpha,\varepsilon,\delta,Q_{0}}\subset\Phi_{\alpha,\infty,Q_{0}}
. Hence, in this non-private regime, it suffices to show

\displaystyle\inf\Bigl\{\rho>0:\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\infty,Q_{0}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}(\rho;Q_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P}[1-\phi]\leq\beta\Big\}\geq C_{\eta}\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\Bigg\},

as the infimum term above is smaller than or equal to
\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{MMD}}
.
To this end, we use the standard Le Cam’s two point method
(
Le Cam, 1973
;
Le Cam, 2012
)
. In particular, for any
P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}(\widetilde{\rho};Q_{0})
with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}=C_{\eta}\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\Bigg\},

we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\infty,Q_{0}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}(\widetilde{\rho};Q_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P}[1-\phi]~\geq~

\displaystyle\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\infty,Q_{0}}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}[1-\phi]=1-\sup_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\infty,Q_{0}}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}[\phi]

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle 1-\alpha-d_{\mathrm{TV}}(P_{0}^{\otimes n},Q_{0}^{\otimes n})

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{\geq}~

\displaystyle 1-\alpha-1+\frac{1}{2}e^{-d_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{0}^{\otimes n}\|Q_{0}^{\otimes n})}~\overset{(\mathrm{ii})}{=}~\frac{1}{2}e^{-n\times d_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{0}\|Q_{0})}-\alpha,

where step (i) holds by Bretagnolle–Huber inequality
(
Canonne, 2022
, Lemma B.4)
and step (ii) uses the chain rule of the KL divergence. Therefore the minimax type II error is bounded by
\beta
, that is

\displaystyle\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\infty,Q_{0}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}(\widetilde{\rho};Q_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P}[1-\phi]\geq\beta,

provided that
\alpha+\beta<0.4
and

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{0}\|Q_{0})\leq\frac{1}{n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{2(\alpha+\beta)}\biggr).

Hence it is enough to find an instance of
(P_{0},Q_{0})
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P_{0},Q_{0})\geq C_{\eta}\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\Bigg\}\quad\text{and}

(32a)

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{0}\|Q_{0})\leq\frac{1}{n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{2(\alpha+\beta)}\biggr).

(32b)

Motivated by the proof of
Tolstikhin et al., 2017
, we pick two discrete distributions
P_{0}=p_{0}\delta_{x}+(1-p_{0})\delta_{v}
and
Q_{0}=q_{0}\delta_{x}+(1-q_{0})\delta_{v}
, where
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
0<p_{0},q_{0}<1
and
\delta_{x}
is a Dirac measure at
x
. In this setting, the calculations in
Tolstikhin et al., 2017
show that

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P_{0},Q_{0})=\sqrt{2(p_{0}-q_{0})^{2}\bigl(\kappa(0)-\kappa(x-v)\bigr)}.

(33)

In addition, under the same setting, the KL divergence of
P_{0}
from
Q_{0}
is bounded by

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{0}\|Q_{0})\leq\frac{(p_{0}-q_{0})^{2}}{q_{0}(1-q_{0})}.

Now set

\displaystyle p_{0}=\frac{1}{2}+\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{1}{4n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{2(\alpha+\beta)}\biggr)},\frac{1}{2}\Bigg\}\quad\text{and}\quad q_{0}=\frac{1}{2},

so that both
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
are valid probability measures, which leads to

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{0}\|Q_{0})\leq\min\Biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{2(\alpha+\beta)}\biggr),\,1\Biggr\}\leq\frac{1}{n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{2(\alpha+\beta)}\biggr).

We also choose
x
and
v
such that
\kappa(0)-\kappa(z)\geq\eta
for
z=x-v
where
\eta
is given in the theorem assumption. In this setting, the explicit expression of the MMD metric (
33
) can be used to show

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P_{0},Q_{0})~\geq~

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\eta}\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{1}{4n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{2(\alpha+\beta)}\biggr)},\,\frac{1}{2}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle C_{\eta}\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\alpha+\beta}\biggr)},\,1\Bigg\}

where the second inequality holds by taking
C_{\eta}
sufficiently small under the condition
\alpha+\beta<0.4
. Hence we have shown that the sufficient conditions in (
32a
) and (
32b
) are fulfilled, and thus the minimax separation satisfies

\displaystyle\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{MMD}}\geq C_{\eta}\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\Bigg\}.

Next we focus on the privacy regime and prove the second term in the lower bound.

E.10.2
Privacy regime

The second term in the lower bound can be proved based on the coupling idea described in
Acharya et al., 2018
and
Acharya et al., 2021
. In detail, we pick
P_{0}
from
\mathcal{P}_{1,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}(\delta;Q_{0})
, and let
(\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{Y}_{n})
be a coupling between
P_{0}^{\otimes n}
and
Q_{0}^{\otimes n}
with
D=\mathbb{E}[d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{Y}_{n})]
. Then for any
\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\varepsilon,Q_{0}}
, the proof of
Acharya et al., 2021
shows

\displaystyle 1-\alpha~\leq~\mathbb{E}_{Q_{0}}[1-\phi]

\displaystyle~\leq~\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}[1-\phi]\cdot e^{10D\varepsilon}+0.1+10D\delta e^{10D\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}[1-\phi]

\displaystyle~\geq~e^{-10D\varepsilon}(0.9-\alpha)-10D\delta.

For completeness, we give details here. By Markov’s inequality, the event
\mathcal{E}^{c}=\{d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{Y}_{n})>10D\}
holds with probability at most
1/10
(here one can replace the constant
10
with some other positive number). Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Q_{0}}[1-\phi]=\mathbb{P}_{Q_{0}}(\phi=0)~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{Q_{0}}(\phi=0\,|\,\mathcal{E})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{Q_{0}}(\phi=0\,|\,\mathcal{E})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})+0.1

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{0}}(\phi=0\,|\,\mathcal{E})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\cdot e^{10D\varepsilon}+0.1+10D\delta e^{(10D-1)\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}[1-\phi]\cdot e^{10D\varepsilon}+0.1+10D\delta e^{10D\varepsilon},

where step
(\dagger)
holds due to the fact that
\phi
is
\varepsilon
-differentially private and inequality (
2
). Letting
E\coloneqq 10D
for notation purposes, and using the fact that
\alpha\in(0,1/5)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\varepsilon,Q_{0}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}(\rho;Q_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P}[1-\phi]~\geq~

\displaystyle\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\varepsilon,Q_{0}}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}[1-\phi]

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle e^{-E\varepsilon}(0.9-\alpha)-E\delta

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle 0.5e^{-E\varepsilon}-E\delta

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle 0.25e^{-E\varepsilon},

provided that the condition
E\delta\leq 0.25e^{-E\varepsilon}
holds.
Hence, we require

\displaystyle\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\varepsilon,Q_{0}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}(\rho;Q_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P}[1-\phi]~\geq~0.25e^{-E\varepsilon}~\geq~\beta,

which is fulfilled when

E~\leq~\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\beta}\biggr)\qquad\text{and}\qquad E\delta~\leq~\frac{1}{4}e^{-E\varepsilon}.

(34)

The second condition, equivalently
E\delta e^{E\varepsilon}\leq 1/4
, is trivial when
\delta=0
. Hence by assuming
\delta>0
, we verify that

\displaystyle E\leq\frac{1}{4(\varepsilon+\delta)}\quad\text{implies}\quad E\delta e^{E\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{4}.

To see this, under the condition
E\leq\frac{1}{4(\varepsilon+\delta)}
and letting
x=\varepsilon/\delta
,

\displaystyle E\delta e^{E\varepsilon}\leq\frac{\delta}{4(\varepsilon+\delta)}e^{\frac{\varepsilon}{4(\varepsilon+\delta)}}=\frac{1}{4(x+1)}e^{\frac{x}{4(x+1)}}

Hence it suffices to show that for all
x>0
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4(x+1)}e^{\frac{x}{4(x+1)}}\leq\frac{1}{4}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad f(x)\coloneqq 4(x+1)\log(x+1)-x\geq 0.

The first derivative of
f
is given as
f^{\prime}(x)=4\log(x+1)+3>0
, which implies that
f
is monotone increasing and
f(x)\geq 0
for all
x>0
.
Hence, for all
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,1)
, the conditions in
Equation

34
are satisfied when

E~\leq~\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\beta}\biggr)\qquad\text{and}\qquad E~\leq~\frac{1}{4(\varepsilon+\delta)},

which are in particular satisfied when

E~\leq~\frac{1}{4(\varepsilon+\delta)}\min\Biggl\{\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\beta}\biggr),1\Biggl\}.

(35)

Furthermore, given
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
,
Acharya et al., 2021
ensures the existence of a coupling between
P_{0}^{\otimes n}
and
Q_{0}^{\otimes n}
such that

\displaystyle D=\mathbb{E}[d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{1}^{n},\mathcal{Y}_{1}^{n})]=\frac{n}{2}\|P_{0}-Q_{0}\|_{1},

so
E=10D=5n\|P_{0}-Q_{0}\|_{1}
. The condition in Equation (
35
) becomes

\|P_{0}-Q_{0}\|_{1}~\leq~\frac{1}{20n(\varepsilon+\delta)}\min\Biggl\{\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\beta}\biggr),1\Biggl\}.

(36)

As in the non-private case, we can pick two discrete distributions
P_{0}=p_{0}\delta_{x}+(1-p_{0})\delta_{v}
and
Q_{0}=q_{0}\delta_{x}+(1-q_{0})\delta_{v}
, where
q_{0}=1/2
and

\displaystyle p_{0}=\frac{1}{2}+\min\Biggl\{\frac{1}{40n(\varepsilon+\delta)}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\beta}\biggr),\,\frac{1}{2}\Biggr\}.

This choice ensures that

\displaystyle\|P_{0}-Q_{0}\|_{1}=2|p_{0}-q_{0}|=2\min\Biggl\{\frac{1}{40n(\varepsilon+\delta)}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\beta}\biggr),\,\frac{1}{2}\Biggr\}.

We also choose
x
and
v
such that
\kappa(0)-\kappa(z)\geq\eta
for
z=x-v
under which

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P_{0},Q_{0})

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{2\eta}\min\Biggl\{\frac{1}{40n(\varepsilon+\delta)}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\beta}\biggr),\,\frac{1}{2}\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\geq C_{\eta}\min\Biggl\{\frac{1}{n(\varepsilon+\delta)}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\beta}\biggr),\,1\Biggr\},

where the second inequality holds under the condition
\beta\in(0,1/5)
. Therefore, under privacy regime, for all
\varepsilon>0
and all
\delta\in[0,1)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{MMD}}\geq C_{\eta}\min\Biggl\{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n(\varepsilon+\delta)},\,1\Biggr\},

which proves the inequality in (
31a
).

E.10.3
Equivalence of rates

We now prove the equivalence in (
31b
) when
\alpha\asymp\beta
.
As previously noted, we have (
31a
)
\geq
(
31b
) as

\log(1/(1-\delta))\geq\delta\quad\textrm{ for all $\delta\in[0,1)$.}

Hence, it remains to show that (
31a
)
\leq
(
31b
), so that, up to constants, we have

\displaystyle\max\Biggl\{\min\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n}},\,1/2\bigg\},\,\min\biggl\{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n(\varepsilon+\delta)},\,1/2\biggr\}\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\star)}{\asymp}~

\displaystyle\max\Biggl\{\min\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n}},\,1/2\bigg\},\,\min\biggl\{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n\big(\varepsilon+\log(1/(1-\delta))\big)},\,1/2\biggr\}\Biggr\}.

Here, we have used the fact that
\alpha\asymp\beta
and have absorbed some terms in the constants to replace the
1
’s with
1/2
’s in the minima.
For the result to be non-trivial we need to assume

\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n}}~<~1/2\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n\big(\varepsilon+\log(1/(1-\delta))\big)}~<~1/2.

(37)

•

If
\delta\in[0,1/2)
, then
2\log(2)\delta\geq\log(1/(1-\delta))\geq\delta
, and so we get

\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n\big(\varepsilon+\log(1/(1-\delta))\big)}~\leq~\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n(\varepsilon+\delta)}~\leq~2\log(2)\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n\big(\varepsilon+\log(1/(1-\delta))\big)}

which proves
(\star)
for
\delta\in[0,1/2)
.

•

If
\delta\in[1/2,1)
, then
\delta\geq 1/2>\sqrt{\log(1/\beta)/n}
, hence we get

\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n\big(\varepsilon+\log(1/(1-\delta))\big)}~\leq~\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n\delta}~<~\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n}}

and

\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n(\varepsilon+\delta)}~<~\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n}},

so both sides of
(\star)
are equal to
\sqrt{{\log(1/\beta)}/{n}}
when
\delta\in[1/2,1)
. So, when
\delta\geq\sqrt{\log(1/\beta)/n}
, the non-DP rate dominates.

We have shown that
(\star)
holds for all
\delta\in[0,1)
, which completes the proof.

E.11
Proof of
Theorem

9

To simplify the notation, we denote the square of the empirical MMD based on
\mathcal{X}_{n+m}
as
V_{\mathrm{MMD}}
(or equivalently the V-statistic of MMD), and that based on permuted data
\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}}
as
V_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}
. We also denote the U-statistic of the MMD as
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}
,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{MMD}}=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j})+\frac{1}{m(m-1)}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Z_{i},Z_{j})-\frac{2}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Z_{j}),

and
U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}
is similarly defined based on
\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}}
. Remark that the difference between the V-statistic and the U-statistic of MMD is

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{MMD}}-U_{\mathrm{MMD}}~=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\lambda_{i}}+\frac{1}{m}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\lambda_{i}}

(38)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j})-\frac{1}{m^{2}(m-1)}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Z_{i},Z_{j}).

Given this connection, our proof strategy is to leverage known results of the U-statistic along with a careful analysis of the difference between
V_{\mathrm{MMD}}
and
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}
. Writing the sensitivity of
\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{MMD}}}
as
\Delta_{V^{1/2}}
,
Lemma

17
ensures that the
\mathrm{dpMMD}
test rejects the null if and only if

\displaystyle\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{MMD}}}+\frac{2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}}{\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\zeta_{0}>q_{1-\alpha,B},

where
q_{1-\alpha,B}
is the
1-\alpha
quantile of
\{\sqrt{V_{\bm{\pi}_{i},\mathrm{MMD}}}+2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{i}\}_{i=0}^{B}
. We then closely follow the proof steps given in
Section

E.4
to prove the claim.

Bounding the type II error.
As in the proof of
Theorem

4
in
Section

E.4
, denote by
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
and
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
, the
1-\alpha/12
quantile of the conditional distribution of
V_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}^{1/2}
given
\mathcal{X}_{n+m}
and that of
2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta
, respectively. Then for
B\geq 6\alpha^{-1}\log(2/\beta)
, the analysis given in the proof of
Theorem

4
shows that the type II error of the
\mathrm{dpMMD}
test can be bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{MMD}}}+2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}\bigr)~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{MMD}}}+2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}+q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}\bigr)+\beta/2

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{MMD}}}\leq q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}+q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}+R_{2}\bigr)+5\beta/8,

where we recall
R_{2}=2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\beta/8)
. We also note that the quantile
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
can be explicitly written as

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}=2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12),

and using the inequality (
28
), the type II error is upper bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{MMD}}}+2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{MMD}}}\leq q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}+14\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\max\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}\bigr)+5\beta/8.

(39)

Given this bound for the type II error, our next effort lies in bounding
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
.

Bounding
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
.
To obtain an upper bound for
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
, we leverage the exponential inequality for permuted U-statistics studied in
Kim et al., 2022a
and
Schrab et al., 2023
. To this end, denote the probability function, which is taken over
\bm{\pi}
conditional on
\mathcal{X}_{m+n}
, as
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}
and note that for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\Biggl(\sqrt{V_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}}\geq\sqrt{\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{m}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\lambda_{i}}+t}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\Biggl(V_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}\geq\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{m}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\lambda_{i}}+t\Biggr)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\Biggl(V_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}-U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}+U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}\geq\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{m}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\lambda_{i}}+t\Biggr)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\Biggl(\biggl\{V_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}-U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}-\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{m}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\lambda_{i}}\biggr\}+U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}\geq t\Biggr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\bigl(U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}\geq t\bigr),

where the last equality holds since for any permutation
\bm{\pi}

\displaystyle V_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}-U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}-\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{m}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\lambda_{i}}\leq 0,

which can be seen from the previous expression of
V_{\mathrm{MMD}}-U_{\mathrm{MMD}}
in (
38
) and non-negativity of
k_{\bm{\lambda}}
. Hence
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
is bounded by

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}\leq\sqrt{\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{m}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\lambda_{i}}+q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{U}},

where
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{U}
denotes the
1-\alpha/12
quantile of the permutation distribution of
U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}
. Now using the exponential bound for
U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{MMD}}
in
Kim et al., 2022a
, and following the proof of
Schrab et al., 2023
, we see that there exists a constant
C_{\tau,\beta,M,d}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{U}\leq C_{\tau,\beta,M,d}\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}\biggr)\geq 1-\beta/8,

under the conditions of
Theorem

9
. For simplicity, let us write

\displaystyle b_{n,m,\bm{\lambda}}=\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{m}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\lambda_{i}}\leq\frac{C_{\tau,d}}{n\lambda_{1}\dots\lambda_{d}}.

(40)

Observe that we have
\max\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}\leq C_{\beta}\log(1/\alpha)
for
\alpha\in(0,e^{-1})
. Putting these pieces together and continuing from (
39
), the type II error is further bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{MMD}}}+2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{MMD}}}\leq\sqrt{b_{n,m,\bm{\lambda}}+C_{\tau,\beta,M,d}\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}}+C_{\beta}\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\log(1/\alpha)\Biggr)+6\beta/8

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Biggl(V_{\mathrm{MMD}}\leq b_{n,m,\bm{\lambda}}+C_{\tau,\beta,M,d}\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}+C_{\beta}^{2}\Delta_{V^{1/2}}^{2}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-2}\log^{2}(1/\alpha)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~+2\sqrt{b_{n,m,\bm{\lambda}}+C_{\tau,\beta,M,d}\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}}C_{\beta}\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\log(1/\alpha)\Biggr)+6\beta/8.

(41)

Connecting
V_{\mathrm{MMD}}
with
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}
.
In order to express the condition for type II error control in terms of the
L_{2}
distance, we make use of the existing results for the U-statistic in
Schrab et al., 2023
. For simplicity, we use the notation
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
to represent constants that may depend on
\tau,\beta,s,R,M,d
. The specific values of these constants may vary in different places. To proceed, let us make an observation that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j})\biggr]~=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\bigl[k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\bigr]=\frac{1}{n}\int\int p(y_{1})p(y_{2})k_{\bm{\lambda}}(y_{1},y_{2})dy_{1}dy_{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{\|p\|_{L_{\infty}}}{n}

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\biggl[\frac{1}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j})\biggr]\leq\frac{C_{1}}{n^{3}}\mathbb{E}[k_{\bm{\lambda}}^{2}(Y_{1},Y_{2})]\leq\frac{C_{2}}{n^{3}\lambda_{1}\dots\lambda_{d}}.

(42)

To explain the last display, note that the U-statistic

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j})

achieves the minimum variance among all unbiased estimators of
k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{1},Y_{2})
, including a linear estimator given as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{2i-1},Y_{2i}).

The variance of the above linear estimator is bounded by
n^{-1}\mathbb{E}[k_{\bm{\lambda}}^{2}(Y_{1},Y_{2})]
, up to a constant factor, thereby the inequality (
42
) holds. A similar calculation shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{m^{2}(m-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{m}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Z_{i},Z_{j})\biggr]\leq\frac{\|p\|_{L_{\infty}}}{n}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\biggl[\frac{1}{m^{2}(m-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{m}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Z_{i},Z_{j})\biggr]\leq\frac{C_{3}}{n^{3}\lambda_{1}\dots\lambda_{d}}.

Based on these results combined with Chebyshev’s inequality, we have the following statement, which holds with probability at least
1-\beta/8
,

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{MMD}}-U_{\mathrm{MMD}}+U_{\mathrm{MMD}}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle b_{n,m,\bm{\lambda}}-\frac{1}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j})-\frac{1}{m^{2}(m-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{m}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Z_{i},Z_{j})+U_{\mathrm{MMD}}

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle b_{n,m,\bm{\lambda}}-\frac{C_{4}}{n}-\frac{C_{5}}{n^{3/2}\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\dots\lambda_{d}}}+U_{\mathrm{MMD}}.

Hence, continuing from (
41
), the type II error is upper bounded by

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Biggl(U_{\mathrm{MMD}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{n}+\frac{C_{2}}{n^{3/2}\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}+\frac{C_{3}\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}+\frac{C_{4}\Delta_{V^{1/2}}^{2}\log^{2}(1/\alpha)}{\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-2}}

(43)

\displaystyle+2C_{5}\sqrt{b_{n,m,\bm{\lambda}}+\frac{C_{6}\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}}\frac{\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\log(1/\alpha)}{\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\Biggr)+7\beta/8.

Recall that we assume
\alpha\in(0,e^{-1})
and
\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\leq 1
. Moreover, for the Gaussian kernel,
Lemma

5
shows that the sensitivity of
\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{MMD}}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\leq\frac{C_{6}}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\dots\lambda_{d}}}.

Using these conditions along with the inequality (
40
) for
b_{n,m\bm{\lambda}}
, the previous bound (
43
) can be upper bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Biggl(U_{\mathrm{MMD}}\leq\frac{C_{1}\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}+\frac{C_{2}\log^{2}(1/\alpha)}{n^{2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{2}}

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+\frac{C_{3}\log(1/\alpha)}{n^{3/2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}+\frac{C_{4}\log^{3/2}(1/\alpha)}{n^{3/2}(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d})^{3/4}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\Biggr)+7\beta/8.

Condition in terms of
L_{2}
distance.
As shown in
Schrab et al., 2023
, a sufficient condition for the first probability term in the above display to be less than
\beta/8
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k_{\bm{\lambda}}}^{2}~\geq~

\displaystyle C_{5}\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[U_{\mathrm{MMD}}]}+\frac{C_{1}\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}+\frac{C_{2}\log^{2}(1/\alpha)}{n^{2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{3}\log(1/\alpha)}{n^{3/2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}+\frac{C_{4}\log^{3/2}(1/\alpha)}{n^{3/2}(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d})^{3/4}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}.

Moreover, writing the difference of two densities as
\psi=p-q
and the convolution of
\psi
and
k_{\bm{\lambda}}
as
\psi\ast k_{\bm{\lambda}}
, the proof of
Schrab et al., 2023
yields that the previous condition is implied by

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{L_{2}}^{2}~\geq~\|\psi-\psi\ast\varphi_{\lambda}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+C_{6}

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}+\frac{\log^{2}(1/\alpha)}{n^{2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{n^{3/2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}+\frac{\log^{3/2}(1/\alpha)}{n^{3/2}(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d})^{3/4}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\Biggr\}.

Lastly, the proof of
Schrab et al., 2023
yields that over the Sobolev ball, a sufficient condition for the previous inequality is

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{L_{2}}^{2}~\geq~C_{7}\Biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{2s}

\displaystyle+\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}+\frac{\log^{2}(1/\alpha)}{n^{2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{n^{3/2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}+\frac{\log^{3/2}(1/\alpha)}{n^{3/2}(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d})^{3/4}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\Biggr\}

as claimed.

E.12
Proof of
Lemma

7

Let
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}^{\prime}
be the U-statistic similarly defined as
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}
by replacing
Y_{1}
with
Y_{1}^{\prime}
. Then it can be seen that

\displaystyle|U_{\mathrm{MMD}}-U_{\mathrm{MMD}}^{\prime}|~=~

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{2}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\big\{k(Y_{1},Y_{j})-k(Y_{1}^{\prime},Y_{j})\big\}-\frac{2}{nm}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\big\{k(Y_{1},Z_{j})-k(Y_{1}^{\prime},Z_{j})\big\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{8K}{n}.

Similarly, the difference is bounded by
8K/n
for other neighboring datasets, and thus the global sensitivity of
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}
is bounded by
8K/n
. Let
\epsilon
be a number between
(0,K)
. For a translation invariant kernel with non-empty level sets, we can ensure the existence of an instance where
Y_{1}=\ldots=Y_{n}
and
Z_{1}=\ldots=Z_{m}
such that
k(Y_{1},Z_{1})=\epsilon_{\star}
for some
\epsilon_{\star}\in[0,\epsilon]
. Moreover, by taking
Y_{1}^{\prime}=Z_{1}
, we have
k(Y_{1},Y_{1}^{\prime})=\epsilon_{\star}
and
k(Y_{1}^{\prime},Z_{1})=K
. Under this setting, the difference between
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}
and
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}^{\prime}
becomes

\displaystyle|U_{\mathrm{MMD}}-U_{\mathrm{MMD}}^{\prime}|=\frac{4(K-\epsilon_{\star})}{n}.

Since
\epsilon
(and so
\epsilon_{\star}
) can be arbitrarily small, we see that the global sensitivity defined with the supremum is lower bounded by
4K/n
. This concludes the claim of
Lemma

7
. This concludes the claim of
Lemma

7
.

E.13
Proof of
Theorem

10

For notational convenience, let us write
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}_{0}})=U_{\mathrm{MMD}}
and
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}_{i}})=U_{\bm{\pi}_{i},\mathrm{MMD}}
for
i\in[B]
. For
\alpha>1/(B+1)
,
Lemma

18
yields that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}^{u}=\mathds{1}\biggl(U_{\mathrm{MMD}}+\frac{2c_{m,n}K}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\zeta_{0}>r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\biggr),

where
\alpha_{\star}=\frac{B+1}{B}\alpha-\frac{1}{B}
and
r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}
is the
1-\alpha_{\star}
quantile of
\{U_{\bm{\pi}_{i},\mathrm{MMD}}+\frac{2c_{m,n}K}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\zeta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}
. Let us denote the V-statistic of MMD as

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{MMD}}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})+\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}k(Z_{i},Z_{j})-\frac{2}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}k(Y_{i},Z_{j}),

which is greater than or equal to
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}
since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\geq\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{i})=K\geq\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})

and similarly

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}k(Z_{i},Z_{j})\geq\frac{1}{m(m-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{m}}k(Z_{i},Z_{j})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}k(Z_{i},Z_{i})=K\geq\frac{1}{m(m-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{m}}k(Z_{i},Z_{j}).

Moreover, since
V_{\mathrm{MMD}}\geq 0
, the U-statistic is lower bounded by
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}\geq U_{\mathrm{MMD}}-V_{\mathrm{MMD}}
. Consider a lower bound for
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}-V_{\mathrm{MMD}}
as

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{MMD}}-V_{\mathrm{MMD}}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m(m-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{m}}k(Z_{i},Z_{j})-\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}k(Z_{i},Z_{j})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\biggl[\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{i})\biggr]

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\biggl[\frac{1}{m(m-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{m}}k(Z_{i},Z_{j})-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}k(Z_{i},Z_{i})\biggr]

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle-\frac{K}{n}-\frac{K}{m}.

Thereby,
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}\geq-\frac{K}{n}-\frac{K}{m}
. We use this observation to lower bound the critical value
r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}
as

\displaystyle r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\geq\frac{2c_{m,n}K}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\big\{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\big\}-\frac{K}{n}-\frac{K}{m},

where
