\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\}
denotes the
1-\alpha_{\star}
quantile of
\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}
. Putting pieces together yields

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}^{u}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\biggl(V_{\mathrm{MMD}}+\frac{2c_{m,n}K}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\zeta_{0}>\frac{2c_{m,n}K}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\big\{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\big\}-\frac{K}{n}-\frac{K}{m}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\biggl(\frac{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}{2c_{m,n}K}V_{\mathrm{MMD}}+\zeta_{0}>\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\big\{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\big\}-\frac{\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}{c_{m,n}}\biggr).

Furthermore, note that the statistic
V_{\mathrm{MMD}}
is the square of the empirical MMD, which satisfies

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{MMD}}=\big\{\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)+R_{m,n}\}^{2},

where
R_{m,n}=O_{P}(n^{-1/2})
due to
Gretton et al., 2012
, recalled in
Lemma

13
.

Let us consider
P_{0},Q_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}
such that
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P_{0},Q_{0})=\varrho_{0}
in the theorem statement. Let
P=P_{0}
, and let
Q
be a mixture distribution
Q=wP_{0}+(1-w)Q_{0}
. Since
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}
is a convex set,
Q
belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}
, and it can be seen that

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)=(1-w)\varrho_{0}.

Now for
\gamma\in(1/2,1)
, we set
w=1-(n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{-\gamma}
. Since
\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}\asymp n^{-1/2-r}
with
r\in(0,1/2)
, we can ensure that
w\in(0,1)
and
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)=(n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta})^{-\gamma}\varrho_{0}
for sufficiently large
n
. Moreover, this pair of distributions
(P,Q)
belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}\!(\rho)
for
\rho
as in (
17
) since

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)=\frac{\varrho_{0}}{\big(n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}\big)^{\gamma}}~\geq~\rho~\asymp~\frac{\log(n)}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\quad

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\big(n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}\big)^{1-\gamma}~\gtrsim~\log(n)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad n^{\frac{(1-2r)(1-\gamma)}{2}}~\gtrsim~\log(n).

Moreover, as
n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}\rightarrow\infty
and
\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}\rightarrow 0
,

\displaystyle\frac{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}{2c_{m,n}K}V_{\mathrm{MMD}}=\frac{1}{2c_{m,n}K}\big\{\sqrt{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)+\sqrt{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}R_{m,n}\}^{2}=o_{P}(1),

where we use the fact that
c_{m,n}\in[4,8]
. Having this observation in place, for any fixed
t>0
, we can take
N_{t}>0
such that for all
n\geq N_{t}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}^{u}]~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\zeta_{0}>\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\big\{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\big\}-\frac{\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}{c_{m,n}}-\frac{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}{2c_{m,n}K}V_{\mathrm{MMD}},

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt\Big|\frac{\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}{c_{m,n}}+\frac{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}{2c_{m,n}K}V_{\mathrm{MMD}}\Big|<t\Bigr)+\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\Big|\frac{\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}{c_{m,n}}+\frac{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}{2c_{m,n}K}V_{\mathrm{MMD}}\Big|\geq t\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\zeta_{0}>\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\big\{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\big\}-t\Bigr)+t

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\zeta_{0}>\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\big\{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\big\}-t\,\Big|\,\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\Bigr)\Bigr]+t

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\zeta_{0}>\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\big\{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\big\}\Bigr)+t\|f_{\zeta}\|_{L_{\infty}}+t,

where
f_{\zeta}
denotes the density function of
\mathsf{Laplace}(0,1)
and the last inequality uses the fact that

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{P}(\zeta>a+b)-\mathbb{P}(\zeta>b)\big|\leq\|f_{\zeta}\|_{L_{\infty}}|a|\quad\text{for all $a,b\in\mathbb{R}$.}

Since
\|f_{\zeta}\|_{L_{\infty}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\zeta_{0}>\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha_{\star}}\big\{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\big\}\Bigr)=\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\zeta_{0}>\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha}\big\{\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\big\}\Bigr)\leq\alpha,

we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{MMD}_{k}}\!(\rho)}\mathbb{E}_{P,Q}[\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}^{u}]\leq\alpha+\frac{3t}{2}.

The result follows as
t
can be made arbitrarily small.

E.14
Proof of
Theorem

11

Let us denote by
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
constants that may depend on
\tau,\beta,s,R,M,d
. Following the proofs of
Theorem

4
and
Theorem

9
along with the sensitivity result for
U_{\mathrm{MMD}}
in
Lemma

7
, we may arrive at the point where the type II error of
\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}^{u}
is upper bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[1-\phi_{\mathrm{dpMMD}}^{u}]\leq\mathbb{P}\biggl(U_{\mathrm{MMD}}\leq\frac{C_{1}\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}+\frac{C_{2}\log(1/\alpha)}{n\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\biggr)+7\beta/8.

We then use the proofs of
Schrab et al., 2023
and
Schrab et al., 2023
, and show that the probability term in the above display is less than or equal to
\beta/8
once

\displaystyle\|p-q\|_{L_{2}}^{2}~\geq~C_{3}\Biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{2s}

\displaystyle+\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}}}+\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{n\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\Biggr\}.

This proves
Theorem

11
.

Appendix F
Proofs for
Appendix

B

This section collects the proofs of the results in
Appendix

B
.

F.1
Proof of
Proposition

1

Focusing on MMD, the square of the empirical MMD satisfies

\displaystyle(n+m)\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m})\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}Z_{i}^{2},

which can be seen by the standard asymptotic theory of V-statistics. See
Fernández and Rivera, 2022
. Moreover, note that the empirical MMD and the Laplace noise are independent. Therefore, the continuous mapping theorem along with Slutsky’s theorem proves the result when
\sqrt{n+m}\sigma\rightarrow\eta\in[0,\infty)
. On the other hand, when
\sqrt{n+m}\sigma\rightarrow\infty
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sigma^{-1}M_{\mathrm{MMD}}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n+m}\,\sigma}}_{=\,o_{P}(1)}\times\underbrace{\sqrt{n+m}\,\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m})}_{=\,O_{P}(1)}+\,\zeta=o_{P}(1)+\zeta.

This proves the results for MMD. The proof for HSIC is completely analogous and thereby omitted, which can be derived by using
Zhang et al., 2018
in place of
Fernández and Rivera, 2022
.

F.2
Proof of
Lemma

8

We first prove the claim by considering two scenarios: (i)
B_{n}
is fixed in
n
and (ii)
B_{n}
increases with
n
, and prove the consistency result. We then turn to the general case of
B_{n}
and complete the proof building on the prior result. Throughout this proof, we often omit the dependence of
n
on
B_{n}
and write it as
B
for simplicity.

F.2.1
Simple cases

Fixed
B
.

Assume that
B
is fixed and
B+1>\alpha^{-1}
or equivalently
\alpha(B+1)\geq 1
. As mentioned in the main text, we simply exploit the union bound to prove the claim for fixed
B
. Concretely,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B+1}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})+1\biggr)\leq\alpha\biggr]~=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})\leq\alpha(B+1)-1\biggr]

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\geq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})\leq 0\biggr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl[W_{0,n}-\max_{i\in[B]}W_{i,n}>0\Bigr]

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\geq}~

\displaystyle 1-\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n}),

where step (i) uses condition
\alpha(B+1)>1
and step (ii) uses de Morgan’s law and the union bound. By the condition that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n})=0
and since
\{W_{0,n}-W_{i,n}\}_{i=2}^{B}
are identically distributed as
W_{0,n}-W_{1,n}
, the lower bound converges to one for fixed
B
and thus the consistency result follows.

Increasing
B
.

Next we consider the case where
B
increases to infinity with
n
. For some
\eta>0
(specified later), define an event
\mathcal{A}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\bigg\{\bigg|\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})-\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n}\,|\,\mathcal{G})\bigg|\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2B}\log\left(\frac{2}{\eta}\right)}\bigg\}.

Since
\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n}),\ldots,\mathds{1}(W_{0}\leq W_{B,n})
are i.i.d. random variables conditional on the sigma field
\mathcal{G}
under the condition of
Lemma

8
, Hoeffding’s inequality yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{c}\,|\,\mathcal{G})\leq\eta.

By taking the expectation over
\mathcal{G}
on both sides, it also holds marginally that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{c})\leq\eta
. Now

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B+1}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})+1\biggr)>\alpha\biggr]

(44)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})>\frac{B+1}{B}\biggl(\alpha-\frac{1}{B+1}\biggr)\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})>\frac{B+1}{B}\biggl(\alpha-\frac{1}{B+1}\biggr),\,\mathcal{A}\biggr]+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n}\,|\,\mathcal{G})+\sqrt{\frac{1}{2B}\log\left(\frac{2}{\eta}\right)}>\frac{B+1}{B}\biggl(\alpha-\frac{1}{B+1}\biggr)\biggr]+\eta.

By taking
\eta=B^{-1}
, we know
\eta\rightarrow 0
(since
B\rightarrow\infty
) and

\displaystyle\frac{B+1}{B}\biggl(\alpha-\frac{1}{B+1}\biggr)-\sqrt{\frac{1}{2B}\log\left(\frac{2}{\eta}\right)}\rightarrow\alpha\quad\text{as $n\rightarrow\infty$.}

Moreover our condition guarantees that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n})=0
. Hence for any
\epsilon>0
, we can take
N
such that statement (i)
\eta<\epsilon/2
and statement (ii)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n}\,|\,\mathcal{G})+\sqrt{\frac{1}{2B}\log\left(\frac{2}{\eta}\right)}>\frac{B+1}{B}\biggl(\alpha-\frac{1}{B+1}\biggr)\bigg]

(45)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n}\,|\,\mathcal{G})>\frac{\alpha}{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\alpha}\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2},

hold for all
n\geq N
. Here step (
\dagger
) holds by Markov’s inequality. Thus for all
n\geq N
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B_{n}+1}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{B_{n}}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})+1\biggr)>\alpha\biggr]<\epsilon.

Since
\epsilon
is arbitrary, the above argument proves the claim that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B_{n}+1}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{B_{n}}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})+1\biggr)\leq\alpha\biggr]=1.

F.2.2
Main proof

We now deal with an arbitrary sequence of
B_{n}
such that
\inf_{n\geq 1}B_{n}+1>\alpha^{-1}
and prove the claim. First note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B+1}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})+1\biggr)>\alpha\biggr]=\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B+1}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})>\biggl(\alpha-\frac{1}{B+1}\biggr)\biggr]

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})>0\biggr]=1-\mathbb{P}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})=0\biggr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 1-\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\mathbb{P}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})=0\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{G}\biggr]\biggr\}=1-\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\{\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}>W_{1,n}\,|\,\mathcal{G})\}^{B}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{ii})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle 1-\{\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}>W_{1,n})\}^{B},

where step (i) uses the condition
\alpha>\frac{1}{B+1}
and step (ii) uses Jensen’s inequality. On the other hand, from the previous results (
44
) and Markov’s inequality with
\eta=B^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B+1}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})+1\biggr)>\alpha\biggr]\leq\frac{1}{B}+\frac{\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n})+\sqrt{\frac{1}{2B}\log(2B)}}{\frac{B+1}{B}(\alpha-\frac{1}{B+1})}.

Thus combining the two bounds yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B+1}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})+1\biggr)>\alpha\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\min\Bigg\{1-\{\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}>W_{1,n})\}^{B},\,\frac{1}{B}+\frac{\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n})+\sqrt{\frac{1}{2B}\log(2B)}}{\frac{B+1}{B}(\alpha-\frac{1}{B+1})}\Bigg\}.

Interestingly, a careful argument shows that the above upper bound can be made independent of
B
. Concretely, write
p_{n}\coloneqq\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}>W_{1,n})
for simplicity, which by assumption tends to
1
as
n
tends to infinity, and let
b_{n}
be a positive sequence that goes to infinity and satisfies
p_{n}^{b_{n}}\rightarrow 1
. For instance, one can take
b_{n}=\sqrt{-\log(1-p_{n})}
. Using this notation in place, observe

\displaystyle\min\Bigg\{1-\{\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}>W_{1,n})\}^{B},\,\frac{1}{B}+\frac{\mathbb{P}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{1,n})+\sqrt{\frac{1}{2B}\log(2B)}}{\frac{B+1}{B}(\alpha-\frac{1}{B+1})}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathds{1}(B<b_{n})\big\{1-p_{n}^{B}\big\}+\mathds{1}(B\geq b_{n})\Bigg\{\frac{1}{B}+\frac{1-p_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{2B}\log(2B)}}{\frac{B+1}{B}(\alpha-\frac{1}{B+1})}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 1-p_{n}^{b_{n}}+\Bigg\{\frac{1}{b_{n}}+\frac{1-p_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{2b_{n}}\log(2b_{n})}}{\frac{b_{n}+1}{b_{n}}(\alpha-\frac{1}{b_{n}+1})}\Bigg\}.

Now the upper bound does not depend on
B
and converges to zero, which is ensured by the condition
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}p_{n}=1
. As a result, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\biggl[\frac{1}{B_{n}+1}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{B_{n}}\mathds{1}(W_{0,n}\leq W_{i,n})+1\biggr)\leq\alpha\biggr]=1,

and complete the proof of
Lemma

8
.

F.3
Proof of
Theorem

12

The proof of
Theorem

12
can follow a similar approach to that of
Theorem

7
by replacing concentration inequalities for MMD statistics (
Lemma

10
and
Lemma

13
) with the corresponding ones for HSIC statistics (
Lemma

12
and
Lemma

14
). Throughout this proof, we denote positive constants that only depend on
K
and
L
by
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},\ldots

Step 1 (Bounding the quantile).
Let us remind that
M_{i}
is defined as
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}_{i}})+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{i}
for
i\in\{0\}\cup[B]
. We first examine the
1-\alpha
quantile of
M_{0},M_{1},\ldots,M_{B}
denoted as
q_{1-\alpha,B}
and establish a high-probability upper bound for
q_{1-\alpha,B}
. Again, the proof strategy is similar to that of
Theorem

7
. With an abuse of notation, denote by
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
and
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
, the
1-\alpha/12
quantile of the conditional distribution of
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})
given
\mathcal{X}_{n+m}
and that of
2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta
, respectively. Following the same lines of the proof of
Theorem

7
, we see that
q_{1-\alpha,B}\leq q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}+q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}
with probability at least
1-\beta/2
under our condition for
B
. Moreover applying
Lemma

12
yields

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\max\bigg\{\log\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,1\bigg\}},

whereas
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{b}=2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12)
. Therefore, with probability at least
1-\beta/2
, it holds that

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha,B}\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\max\bigg\{\log\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,1\bigg\}}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12).

(46)

Step 2 (Bounding the type II error).
For the empirical HSIC,
Lemma

14
shows that the following event

\displaystyle E_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\biggl\{\bigg|\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ})-\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n})\bigg|\leq C_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(8/\beta)}{n}}\biggr\}

holds with probability at least
1-\beta/4
. On the other hand, the condition
\zeta_{0}\sim\mathsf{Laplace}(0,1)
ensures that the probability of the event

\displaystyle E_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq\big\{2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}>2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F^{-1}_{\zeta}(\beta/4)\big\}

is equal to
1-\beta/4
where
F_{\zeta}^{-1}
is the inverse cumulative distribution function of
\zeta
. Using these results as well as inequality (
46
) from Step 1, it can be seen in a way similar to the proof of
Theorem

7
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[1-\phi_{\mathrm{dpHSIC}}]~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ})\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}+C_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(8/\beta)}{n}}-2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F^{-1}_{\zeta}(\beta/4)\biggr)+\frac{\beta}{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ})\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\max\bigg\{\log\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,1\bigg\}}

\displaystyle~~~~~+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F_{\zeta}^{-1}(1-\alpha/12)+C_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(8/\beta)}{n}}-2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}F^{-1}_{\zeta}(\beta/4)\biggr)+\beta.

Next, recall that we assume
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\beta\in(0,1-\alpha)
under which it holds

\displaystyle\max\bigg\{\log\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,1,\,\log\biggl(\frac{8}{\beta}\biggr)\bigg\}~\leq~C_{3}\max\bigg\{\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\beta}\biggr)\bigg\}.

(47)

To see this inequality, first assume that
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
. Then

\displaystyle\max\bigg\{\log\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,1\bigg\}~\leq~

\displaystyle C_{4}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\alpha}\biggr)\leq C_{4}\max\biggl\{\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log\biggl(\frac{8}{\beta}\biggr)\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C_{5}\max\biggl\{\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\beta}\biggr)\biggr\}

as
\beta\in(0,1-\alpha)
.
On the other hand, if
\alpha\in[1/2,1)
,
\beta
should be less than
1/2
, which implies that

\displaystyle\max\bigg\{\log\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{12}{\alpha}\biggr),\,1\bigg\}~\leq~

\displaystyle C_{6}\log\biggl(\frac{8}{\beta}\biggr)\leq C_{7}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\beta}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C_{7}\max\biggl\{\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\alpha}\biggr),\,\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\beta}\biggr)\biggr\}.

Therefore inequality (
47
) follows.

Having these ingredients in place, we follow the same lines as in the proof of
Theorem

7
and see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[1-\phi_{\mathrm{dpHSIC}}]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ})\leq C_{8}\sqrt{\frac{\max\bigl\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}}{n}}+C_{9}\frac{\max\bigl\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}}{n\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\biggr)+\beta

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\beta,

where the last inequality holds by taking
C_{K,L}
to be larger than, for instance,
2\max\{C_{8},C_{9}\}+1
in the theorem statement. We conclude the proof by noting that the upper bound is independent of
P_{YZ}
and taking the supremum on both sides over
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}}\!(\rho)
.

F.4
Proof of
Theorem

13

The proof of
Theorem

13
is based on the same idea as that of
Theorem

8
, which uses Le Cam’s two point method and coupling method. Therefore, we omit the details explained in the proof of
Theorem

8
, and instead focus on key differences. As in the proof of
Theorem

8
, we simply let
\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{HSIC}}=\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\alpha,\beta,\varepsilon,\delta,n)
and establish the minimax separation by examining the non-privacy regime and privacy regime in order.

F.4.1
Non-privacy regime

We begin by proving that
\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{HSIC}}\geq C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\min\{\sqrt{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))/n},1\}
in the non-privacy regime. We pick one distribution
P_{YZ,0}
from
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}}(\widetilde{\rho})
with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}=C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\min\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\bigg\}

and denote the product of its marginals as
P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0}
. Then, as in
Section

E.10.1
, an application of Le Cam’s two point method
(
Le Cam, 1973
;
Le Cam, 2012
)
and Bretagnolle–Huber inequality
(
Canonne, 2022
, Lemma B.4)
yields

\displaystyle\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\varepsilon,\delta}}\sup_{P_{YZ}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}}(\widetilde{\rho})}\mathbb{E}_{P_{YZ}^{n}}[1-\phi]~\geq~

\displaystyle\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\infty}}\sup_{P_{YZ}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}}(\widetilde{\rho})}\mathbb{E}_{P_{YZ}}[1-\phi]

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle\inf_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\infty}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{YZ,0}}[1-\phi]=1-\sup_{\phi\in\Phi_{\alpha,\infty}}\mathbb{E}_{P_{YZ,0}}[\phi]

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle 1-\alpha-d_{\mathrm{TV}}(P_{YZ,0}^{\otimes n},P_{Y,0}^{\otimes n}P_{Z,0}^{\otimes n})

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}e^{-n\times d_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{YZ,0}\|P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0})}-\alpha.

Therefore the minimax type II error is at least
\beta
if
\alpha+\beta<0.4
as well as

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{YZ,0}\|P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0})\leq\frac{1}{n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{2(\alpha+\beta)}\biggr).

Hence
\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{HSIC}}\geq C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\min\{\sqrt{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))/n},1\}
follows if we find
P_{YZ,0}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ,0},P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0})\geq C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\Bigg\}\quad\text{and}

(48a)

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{YZ,0}\|P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0})\leq\frac{1}{n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{2(\alpha+\beta)}\biggr).

(48b)

To this end, consider (discrete) random vectors
Y\in\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
and
Z\in\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
where
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{Y}}
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{Z}}
. Further assume that
\mathbb{P}(Y=y_{1})=\mathbb{P}(Y=y_{2})=1/2
and
\mathbb{P}(Z=z_{1})=\mathbb{P}(Z=z_{2})=1/2
. Suppose that the joint probabilities of
(Y,Z)
are given as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Y=y_{1},Z=z_{1})=\mathbb{P}(Y=y_{2},Z=z_{2})=1/4+\nu\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Y=y_{1},Z=z_{2})=\mathbb{P}(Y=y_{2},Z=z_{1})=1/4-\nu,

where
\nu\in(0,1/4]
. In this setting, it can be seen that
P_{YZ,0}\neq P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0}
and thus
Y
and
Z
are trivially dependent. For such
P_{YZ,0}
and translation invariant kernels, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ,0})~\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{=}~

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Y_{2})\ell(Z_{1},Z_{2})]+\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Y_{2})\ell(Z_{3},Z_{4})]-2\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Y_{2})\ell(Z_{1},Z_{3})]}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{=}~

\displaystyle\sqrt{4\nu^{2}\{\kappa_{Y}(0)-\kappa_{Y}(y_{1}-y_{2})\}\{\kappa_{Z}(0)-\kappa_{Z}(z_{1}-z_{2})\}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(iii)}}{\geq}~

\displaystyle\sqrt{4\nu^{2}\eta_{Y}\eta_{Z}}=2\nu\sqrt{\eta_{Y}\eta_{Z}},

where step (i) follows by
Gretton et al., 2005
with
\{(Y_{i},Z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{4}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P_{YZ,0}
, step (ii) can be verified through algebra and the last inequality (iii) holds by choosing
(y_{1},y_{2})
and
(z_{1},z_{2})
such that
y_{1}-y_{2}=y_{0}
and
z_{1}-z_{2}=z_{0}
.

On the other hand, we verify condition (
48b
) based on the well-known fact
(
e.g.
,
Tsybakov, 2009
, Lemma 2.7)
that the Kullback–Leibler divergence is upper bounded by
\chi^{2}
divergence denoted by
d_{\chi^{2}}(P_{YZ,0}\|P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0})
. This gives

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{YZ,0}\|P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0})~\leq~

\displaystyle d_{\chi^{2}}(P_{YZ,0}\|P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0})=8\biggl(\frac{1}{4}+\nu\biggr)^{2}+8\biggl(\frac{1}{4}-\nu\biggr)^{2}-1

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 16\nu^{2}.

Using the above inequality, condition (
48b
) is fulfilled by taking

\displaystyle\nu=\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{1}{16n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{2(\alpha+\beta)}\biggr)},\,\frac{1}{4}\Bigg\}

for which condition (
48a
) is also satisfied as

\displaystyle\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ,0})~\geq~

\displaystyle 2\nu\sqrt{\eta_{Y}\eta_{Z}}=\frac{\sqrt{\eta_{Y}\eta_{Z}}}{2}\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{2(\alpha+\beta)}\biggr)},\,1\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\min\Bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\Bigg\}

as
\alpha+\beta<0.4
.
Therefore it holds that
\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{HSIC}}\geq C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\min\{\sqrt{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))/n},1\}
as desired.

F.4.2
Privacy regime

The proof of the separation rate under the privacy regime is essentially the same as that for the
\mathrm{dpMMD}
test in Appendix
E.10.2
. All we need is to find an instance of
P_{YZ,0}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ,0})\geq C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\min\Bigg\{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n\big(\varepsilon+\delta\big)},\,1\Bigg\}\quad\text{and}

(49a)

\displaystyle\|P_{YZ,0}-P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0}\|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{20n\big(\varepsilon+\delta\big)}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\beta}\biggr).

(49b)

To this end, choose the distribution of
(Y,Z)
as in the case of the non-privacy regime. For such
P_{YZ,0}
, a direct calculation gives
\|P_{YZ,0}-P_{Y,0}P_{Z,0}\|_{1}=4\nu
and thus condition (
49b
) is satisfied if we take

\displaystyle\nu=\min\Bigg\{\frac{1}{80n\big(\varepsilon+\delta\big)}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\beta}\biggr),\,\frac{1}{4}\Bigg\}.

On the other hand, the previous calculation of the lower bound for
\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ,0})
yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ,0})~\geq~

\displaystyle 2\nu\sqrt{\eta_{Y}\eta_{Z}}=2\min\Bigg\{\frac{1}{80n\big(\varepsilon+\delta\big)}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\beta}\biggr),\,\frac{1}{4}\Bigg\}\sqrt{\eta_{Y}\eta_{Z}}

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\min\Bigg\{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n\big(\varepsilon+\delta\big)},\,1\Bigg\}

which in turn verifies condition (
49a
) under
\beta\in(0,1/5)
. Therefore it holds that the minimax separation under privacy regime
\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{HSIC}}\geq C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\min\!\big\{\log(1/\beta)/(n(\varepsilon+\delta)),1\big\}
.

F.4.3
Combining bounds

Combining the lower bounds for the non-privacy and privacy regimes, for all
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,1)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\rho^{\star}_{\mathrm{HSIC}}~\geq~

\displaystyle C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\max\Biggl\{\min\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\bigg\},\,\min\biggl\{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n(\varepsilon+\delta)},\,1\biggr\}\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle C_{\eta_{Y},\eta_{Z}}\max\Biggl\{\min\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/(\alpha+\beta))}{n}},\,1\bigg\},\,\min\biggl\{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{n\big(\varepsilon+\log(1/(1-\delta))\big)},\,1\biggr\}\Biggr\}.

The last inequality holds since
\log(1/(1-\delta))\geq\delta
for all
\delta\in[0,1)
, and is actually tight when
\alpha\asymp\beta
as explained in
Section

E.10.3
. This concludes the proof of
Theorem

12
.

F.5
Proof of
Theorem

14

The proof of
Theorem

14
follows the same structure as the proof of
Theorem

9
in
Section

E.11
. For simplicity, write
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}
given in (
12
) with the Gaussian kernels as
V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
and similarly the U-statistic given in (
21
) with the Gaussian kernel as
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
. We also denote the corresponding statistics based on permuted data
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}}
as
V_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}
and
U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}
, respectively. Letting

\displaystyle K=\prod_{i=1}^{d_{Y}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\lambda_{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad L=\prod_{i=1}^{d_{Z}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\mu_{i}},

Lemma

22
ensures that the difference between
V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
and
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
can be written as

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}-U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}=D_{1}+D_{2},

where

\displaystyle D_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{n-1}{n^{2}}KL-\frac{L}{n^{3}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j})-\frac{K}{n^{3}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{i},Z_{j}),

\displaystyle D_{2}=

\displaystyle-\frac{3n^{2}-4n+2}{(n-1)n^{4}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{i},Z_{j})+\frac{2(5n^{2}-8n+4)}{n^{4}(n-1)(n-2)}\sum_{(i,j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{3}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{i},Z_{j_{2}})

\displaystyle-\frac{6n^{2}-11n+6}{n^{4}(n-1)(n-2)(n-3)}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{4}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{j_{1}},Z_{j_{2}}).

The term
D_{1}
is invariant to any permutation of
Z
values, and thus the permutation distribution of
V_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}
is equivalent to the permutation distribution of
U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}+D_{1}+D_{2,\bm{\pi}}
where
D_{2,\bm{\pi}}
has the same form of
D_{2}
but computed based on permuted data
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}}
. Writing the sensitivity of
\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}}
as
\Delta_{V^{1/2}}
,
Lemma

17
yields that the
\mathrm{dpHSIC}
test rejects the null if and only if

\displaystyle\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}}+\frac{2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}}{\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\zeta_{0}>q_{1-\alpha,B},

where
q_{1-\alpha,B}
is the
1-\alpha
quantile of
\{\sqrt{V_{\bm{\pi}_{i},\mathrm{HSIC}}}+2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{i}\}_{i=0}^{B}
. As in the proof of
Theorem

9
in
Section

E.11
, we let
q_{1-\alpha,\infty}^{a}
denote the
1-\alpha
quantile of the conditional distribution of
V_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}^{1/2}
given
\mathcal{X}_{n}
. Then under the condition on
B
, the analysis given in the proof of
Theorem

9
shows that the type II error of the
\mathrm{dpHSIC}
test can be bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}}+2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}}\leq q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}+14\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\max\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}\bigr)+5\beta/8.

(50)

We next delve into
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
, and then continue to upper bound the type II error. In what follows, we use the notation
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},\ldots
to represent constants, which could be dependent on
\alpha,\beta,s,R,M,d_{Y},d_{Z}
. The values of these constants may vary in different places.

Bounding
q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}
.
Using the relationship between
V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
and
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
in
Lemma

22
and the quantile inequality in
Lemma

20
, we have

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}~=~

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha/12}\bigl(\big\{U_{\bm{\pi}_{i},\mathrm{HSIC}}+D_{2,\bm{\pi}_{i}}\big\}_{i=0}^{\infty}+D_{1}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha/24}\bigl(\big\{U_{\bm{\pi}_{i},\mathrm{HSIC}}\big\}_{i=0}^{\infty}\bigr)+\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha/24}\bigl(\big\{D_{2,\bm{\pi}_{i}}\big\}_{i=0}^{\infty}\bigr)+D_{1}}.

The quantile of the permuted U-statistic
U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}
has been studied by
Kim et al., 2022a
. In particular, the proof of
Kim et al., 2022a
along with the proof of
Albert et al., 2022
shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\pi}}[U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}]=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{\bm{\pi}}\bigl(U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\bigr)\bigr]\leq\frac{C_{1}}{n^{2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}}.

Thus by Chebyshev’s inequality, it can be seen that the following inequality holds with probability at least
1-\beta/8
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha/24}\bigl(\big\{U_{\bm{\pi}_{i},\mathrm{HSIC}}\big\}_{i=0}^{\infty}\bigr)\leq\frac{C_{2}}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}}},

(51)

For the
1-\alpha/24
quantile of
\big\{D_{2,\bm{\pi}_{i}}\big\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
, note that

\displaystyle D_{2,\bm{\pi}}\leq\frac{C_{3}}{n^{4}}\sum_{(i,j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{3}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j_{2}}})

due to the non-negativity of
k_{\bm{\lambda}}
and
\ell_{\bm{\mu}}
, and also note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\pi}}\Biggl[\sum_{(i,j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{3}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j_{2}}})\,\Big|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\Biggr]\leq\frac{C_{4}}{n}\Biggl[\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})\Biggr]\Biggl[\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{j_{1}},Z_{j_{2}})\Biggr].

Then Markov’s inequality yields that the
1-\alpha/24
quantile of
\big\{D_{2,\bm{\pi}_{i}}\big\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha/8}\bigl(\big\{D_{2,\bm{\pi}_{i}}\big\}_{i=0}^{\infty}\bigr)\leq\frac{C_{5}}{n^{5}}\Biggl[\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})\Biggr]\Biggl[\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{j_{1}},Z_{j_{2}})\Biggr].

Moreover, since we assume
\|p_{YZ}\|_{L_{\infty}}\leq M
and
\|p_{Y}p_{Z}\|_{L_{\infty}}\leq M
,

\displaystyle\max\Big\{\mathbb{E}[k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{1},Z_{2})],\ \mathbb{E}[k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{1},Z_{3})],\ \mathbb{E}[k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{3},Z_{4})]\Big\}\leq C_{6}.

For instance, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{1},Y_{2})\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{1},Z_{2})]~=~

\displaystyle\int\cdots\int k_{\bm{\lambda}}(y_{1},y_{2})\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(z_{1},z_{2})p_{YZ}(y_{1},z_{1})p_{YZ}(y_{2},z_{2})\mathrm{d}y_{1}\mathrm{d}y_{2}\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathrm{d}z_{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\|p_{YZ}\|_{L_{\infty}}\int\int\underbrace{\int k_{\bm{\lambda}}(y_{1},y_{2})\mathrm{d}y_{1}}_{=1}\underbrace{\int\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(z_{1},z_{2})\mathrm{d}z_{1}}_{=1}p_{YZ}(y_{2},z_{2})\mathrm{d}y_{2}\mathrm{d}z_{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle M,

and the other terms can be similarly analyzed. Given this ingredient, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Biggl\{\Biggl[\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k_{\bm{\lambda}}(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})\Biggr]\Biggl[\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}\ell_{\bm{\mu}}(Z_{j_{1}},Z_{j_{2}})\Biggr]\Biggr\}\leq C_{7}n^{4}.

Therefore, another application of Markov’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha/24}\bigl(\big\{D_{2,\bm{\pi}_{i}}\big\}_{i=0}^{\infty}\bigr)\leq\frac{C_{8}}{n},

(52)

with probability at least
1-\beta/8
, and observe that

D_{1}\leq\frac{n-1}{n^{2}}KL\leq C_{9}/\{n\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\}
.

In summary, with probability at least
1-\beta/4
,

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}\leq\frac{C_{10}}{\sqrt{n\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}}}.

(53)

given that
D_{1}\leq KL/n
and
\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\leq 1
.

Bounding the type II error.
We continue from (
50
), and note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}}\leq q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}+14\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\max\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}\leq(q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a})^{2}+14^{2}\Delta_{V^{1/2}}^{2}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-2}\max\{\log^{2}(1/\alpha),\log^{2}(1/\beta)\}

\displaystyle~~~+28q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\max\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}\leq\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha/24}\bigl(\big\{U_{\bm{\pi}_{i},\mathrm{HSIC}}\big\}_{i=0}^{\infty}\bigr)+\mathrm{Quantile}_{1-\alpha/24}\bigl(\big\{D_{2,\bm{\pi}_{i}}\big\}_{i=0}^{\infty}\bigr)-D_{2}

\displaystyle~~~+14^{2}\Delta_{V^{1/2}}^{2}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-2}\max\{\log^{2}(1/\alpha),\log^{2}(1/\beta)\}+28q_{1-\alpha/12,\infty}^{a}\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\max\{\log(1/\alpha),\log(1/\beta)\}\Bigr),

where the first equality simply follows by taking the square on both sides, and the second equality uses the identity
V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}=U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}+D_{1}+D_{2}
from
Lemma

22
. Next, by
Lemma

6
, the global sensitivity of
\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}}
is bounded as

\displaystyle\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}}}.

In addition, observe that
|D_{2}|\leq C_{2}n^{-1}
with probability at least
1-\beta/16
, which can be proven by Markov’s inequality. Therefore, equipped with the previous ingredients (
50
), (
51
), (
52
) and (
53
), the type II error bound can be bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}}+2\Delta_{V^{1/2}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{0}\leq q_{1-\alpha,B}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}\leq\frac{C_{3}}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}}}+\frac{C_{4}}{n^{2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{2}}

\displaystyle~~~~~~~+\frac{C_{5}}{n^{3/2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\biggr)+\frac{15}{16}\beta.

Condition in terms of
L_{2}
distance.
A slight modification of
Albert et al., 2022
yields that a sufficient condition for the first probability in the above display to be less than
\beta/16
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{HSIC}^{2}_{k_{\bm{\lambda}}\otimes\ell_{\bm{\mu}}}~\geq~

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}]}+\frac{C_{3}}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}}}+\frac{C_{4}}{n^{2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{5}}{n^{3/2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}.

In addition, by writing
\psi=p_{YZ}-p_{Y}p_{Z}
and the convolution of
\psi
and
k_{\bm{\lambda}}\otimes\ell_{\bm{\mu}}
as
\psi\ast(k_{\bm{\lambda}}\otimes\ell_{\bm{\mu}})
,
Albert et al., 2022
show that the previous condition is implied by

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{L_{2}}^{2}~\geq~\|\psi-\psi\ast(k_{\bm{\lambda}}\otimes\ell_{\bm{\mu}})\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+C_{6}

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\frac{1}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}}}+\frac{1}{n^{2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n^{3/2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\Bigg\}.

Finally the proof of
(
Albert et al., 2022
, Theorem 2)
yields that over the Sobolev ball, a sufficient condition for the previous inequality is

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{L_{2}}^{2}~\geq~C_{7}\Bigg\{

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d_{Y}}\lambda_{i}^{2s}+\sum_{i=1}^{d_{Z}}\mu_{i}^{2s}+\frac{1}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n^{2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{2}}+\frac{1}{n^{3/2}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\Bigg\}

as claimed.

F.6
Proof of
Lemma

9

Sensitivity upper bound.

For simplicity, write
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}=U_{\mathrm{HSIC},a}+U_{\mathrm{HSIC},b}-2U_{\mathrm{HSIC},c}
where

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{HSIC},a}~:=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j}),

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{HSIC},b}~:=~

\displaystyle\frac{(n-4)!}{n!}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{4}^{n}}k(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{i_{2}},Z_{j_{2}}),

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{HSIC},c}~:=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n(n-1)(n-2)}\sum_{(i,j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{3}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j_{2}}).

To establish an upper bound for the global sensitivity, we first consider a neighboring dataset
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}=\{(Y_{1}^{\prime},Z_{1}^{\prime}),(Y_{2},Z_{2}),\ldots,(Y_{n},Z_{n})\}
and denote the U-statistic of HSIC based on
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
as
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{\prime}:=U_{\mathrm{HSIC},a}^{\prime}+U_{\mathrm{HSIC},b}^{\prime}-2U_{\mathrm{HSIC},c}^{\prime}
. By the triangle inequality, the absolute deviation between
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
and
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{\prime}
is then bounded as

\displaystyle|U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}-U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{\prime}|~\leq~

\displaystyle|U_{\mathrm{HSIC},a}-U_{\mathrm{HSIC},a}^{\prime}|+|U_{\mathrm{HSIC},b}-U_{\mathrm{HSIC},b}^{\prime}|+2|U_{\mathrm{HSIC},c}-U_{\mathrm{HSIC},c}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{C_{1}KL}{n},

where
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
denote some universal positive constants throughout.

For another neighboring dataset
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}=\{(Y_{1}^{\prime},Z_{1}),(Y_{2},Z_{2}^{\prime}),\ldots,(Y_{n},Z_{n})\}
, a similar analysis shows that
|U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}-U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{\prime}|\leq\frac{C_{2}KL}{n}
. Since
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
is invariant to the permutation of the paired indices, we may conclude that

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{\pi}\in\bm{\Pi}_{n}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}:\\
d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n})\leq 1\end{subarray}}\bigl|U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\bigr|\leq\frac{C_{3}KL}{n}.

(54)

While it is loose, the inequality holds with
C_{3}=24
for
n\geq 4
.

Sensitivity lower bound.

We now show that this upper bound is tight up to a constant factor. We treat the cases of
n
being even or odd separately, and follow a similar reasoning to the one in
Section

E.7
. Recall that kernels
k
and
\ell
are assumed to have non-empty level sets on
\mathbb{Y}
and
\mathbb{Z}
. Hence, for a given
\epsilon\in(0,\min\{K,L\})
, we may assume that there exist
y_{a},y_{b}\in\mathbb{Y}
and
z_{a},z_{b}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
k(y_{a},y_{b})=\epsilon_{1}
and
\ell(z_{a},z_{b})=\epsilon_{2}
where
0\leq\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\leq\epsilon
.

Sensitivity lower bound for
n=2k
even.

We begin by considering the case where
n
is even, and compute the difference between U-statistics of the HSIC based on two specific neighboring datasets given below:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix}.

The indices displayed above are
1,\dots,k-2,k-1,k,k+1,\dots 2k
, and it is clear that
d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n})=1
. We further consider the permutation
\bm{\pi}
which permutes only the
k-1
and
k
entries,
i.e.
,
\bm{\pi}=(1,2,\ldots,k-2,k,k-1,k+1,\ldots,n)
, so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{b}\\
y_{b}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix}.

We now compute the U-statistic of the HSIC based on these two permuted datasets. Unfortunately, a direct computation of
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
requires a complicated case-by-case analysis. Instead, we consider the following trick to simplify calculations. First of all, we pretend that
(y_{a},z_{a})
and
(y_{b},z_{b})
take some specific values, say
(y_{a},z_{a})=(1,1)
and
(y_{b},z_{b})=(0,0)
, and consider indicator kernels
k(y,y^{\prime})=K\times\mathds{1}(y=y^{\prime})
and
\ell(z,z^{\prime})=L\times\mathds{1}(z=z^{\prime})
. Under this simplified setting,
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
is essentially the statistic considered in
Kim et al., 2023
for multinomial data, which can be computed in a straightforward manner. In addition, we observe that
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
based on the indicator kernels remains the same as
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
based on generic kernels
k
and
\ell
up to some quantities tending to zero as
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0
. Using this trick along with the observation that
k(y,y)=K
and
\ell(z,z)=L
for all
y\in\mathbb{Y}
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}
, it can be seen that for all
k\geq 2

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})=\frac{k(k-1)}{(2k-1)(2k-3)}KL+C_{1}\epsilon_{1}+C_{2}\epsilon_{2}+C_{3}\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},

where
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
are constants that only depend on
K,L,n
. A similar calculation yields

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})~=~\frac{(k-2)(k^{2}-4k+1)}{(k-1)(2k-1)(2k-3)}KL+C_{1}^{\prime}\epsilon_{1}+C_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon_{2}+C_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},

where
C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime},C_{3}^{\prime}
are constants that only depend on
K,L,n
. From these results, we deduce that

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2}{k-1}-\frac{1}{2k-1}-\frac{1}{2k-3}\right)KL+(C_{1}-C_{1}^{\prime})\epsilon_{1}+(C_{2}-C_{2}^{\prime})\epsilon_{2}+(C_{3}-C_{3}^{\prime})\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(\star)}{\to}~

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2}{k-1}-\frac{1}{2k-1}-\frac{1}{2k-3}\right)KL\geq\frac{KL}{k}

where convergence (
\star
) holds as
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0
for each fixed
K,L,n
, and the last inequality holds for all
k\geq 2
. Therefore, by letting
k=n/2\geq 2
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{\pi}\in\bm{\Pi}_{n}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}:\\
d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n})\leq 1\end{subarray}}\bigl|U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\bigr|\geq\frac{2KL}{n}.

Sensitivity lower bound for
n=2k+1
odd.

We now consider the same two datasets as in the even case but with an additional row consisting of
(y_{b},z_{b})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix},

where the indices displayed are
1,\dots,k-2,k-1,k,k+1,\dots 2k,2k+1
.
As before, we indeed have
d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n})=1
. Once again, we consider the permutation
\bm{\pi}
which permutes only the
k-1
and
k
entries, which leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{a}&z_{a}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{a}&z_{a}\\
y_{a}&z_{b}\\
y_{b}&z_{a}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\\
y_{b}&z_{b}\end{bmatrix}.

To compute
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})
and
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})
, we use the trick considered in the even case, and find that

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})~=~\frac{k(k+1)}{4k^{2}-1}KL+C_{1}\epsilon_{1}+C_{2}\epsilon_{2}+C_{3}\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}

and

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})~=~\frac{(k+1)(k-2)(k^{2}-3k-2)}{k(k-1)(2k-1)(2k+1)}KL+C_{1}^{\prime}\epsilon_{1}+C_{2}^{\prime}\epsilon_{2}+C_{3}^{\prime}\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2},

where
C_{1},\ldots,C_{3}^{\prime}
are constants that only depend on
K,L,n
. As a result, we have

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})

\displaystyle~=~\frac{4(k^{3}-2k+1)}{k(k-1)(4k^{2}-1)}KL+(C_{1}-C_{1}^{\prime})\epsilon_{1}+(C_{2}-C_{2}^{\prime})\epsilon_{2}+(C_{3}-C_{3}^{\prime})\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}

\displaystyle~\overset{(\star)}{\to}~\frac{4(k^{3}-2k+1)}{k(k-1)(4k^{2}-1)}KL\geq\frac{2}{2k+1}KL,

where convergence (
\star
) holds as
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\rightarrow 0
for each fixed
K,L,n
, and the last inequality holds for all
k\geq 2
. Therefore we conclude that

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{\pi}\in\bm{\Pi}_{n}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}:\\
d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n})\leq 1\end{subarray}}\bigl|U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\bigr|\geq\frac{2KL}{n},

for any
n\geq 4
(either even or odd). This completes the derivation of the lower bound on the sensitivity of the HSIC U-statistic. Together with the upper bound (
54
), this verifies the claim of
Lemma

9
.

F.7
Proof of
Theorem

15

Let us write the V-statistic of HSIC as

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j})+\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{n}k(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{i_{2}},Z_{j_{2}})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n^{3}}\sum_{i,j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j_{2}})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{HSIC},a}+V_{\mathrm{HSIC},b}-2V_{\mathrm{HSIC},c},

which is equivalent to the square of
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}
. Using the bounded kernel property, it can be seen that

\displaystyle\max\Big\{|U_{\mathrm{HSIC},a}-V_{\mathrm{HSIC},a}|,\ |U_{\mathrm{HSIC},b}-V_{\mathrm{HSIC},b}|,\ |U_{\mathrm{HSIC},c}-V_{\mathrm{HSIC},c}|\Big\}\leq C_{1}\frac{KL}{n}

for some constant
C_{1}>0
, which leads to

\displaystyle|U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}-V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}|\leq C_{2}\frac{KL}{n}.

Again,
C_{2}
is some positive number. By treating
K
and
L
as some fixed numbers, we also note that
Lemma

14
yields

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}=\big\{\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ})+R_{n}\big\}^{2}\quad\text{where $R_{n}=O_{P}(n^{-1/2})$.}

Having these ingredients in place, we can essentially follow the same proof strategy as for
Theorem

10
and conclude that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}}\!(\rho)}\mathbb{E}_{P_{YZ}}[\phi_{\mathrm{dpHSIC}}^{u}]\leq\alpha.

This completes the proof of
Theorem

15
.

F.8
Proof of
Theorem

16

Let us denote by
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
constants that may depend on
\alpha,\beta,R,M,d_{Y},d_{Z}
. Following the proofs of
Theorem

4
and
Theorem

14
along with the sensitivity result for
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
in
Lemma

9
, we may arrive at the point where the type II error of
\phi_{\mathrm{dpHSIC}}^{u}
is upper bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[1-\phi_{\mathrm{dpHSIC}}^{u}]\leq\mathbb{P}\biggl(U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}}}+\frac{C_{2}}{n\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\biggr)+\frac{15}{16}\beta

We then use the proofs of
Albert et al., 2022
and
Albert et al., 2022
, and show that the probability term in the above display is less than or equal to
\beta/16
once

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{L_{2}}^{2}~\geq~C_{3}\Biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{d_{Y}}\lambda_{i}^{2s}+\sum_{i=1}^{d_{Z}}\mu_{i}^{2s}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n\sqrt{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}}}+\frac{1}{n\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{d_{Y}}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d_{Z}}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}}\Bigg\}.

This proves
Theorem

11
.

Appendix G
Technical Lemmas

This section collects some technical lemmas used in the main proofs. The first lemma from
Kim, 2021
provides an exponential concentration inequality for the permuted MMD statistic with kernel
k
.

Lemma 10

(Theorem 5.1 of
Kim, 2021
)
.

Consider two samples
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
and
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{m}
and write
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}\}=\{Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n},Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{m}\}
. Let us further write
\widetilde{k}(x,y)=k(x,x)+k(y,y)-2k(x,y)
and
\gamma=nm/(n+m)^{2}
. Define

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\bm{i}_{2}^{N}}\widetilde{k}(X_{i},X_{j}).

Then for all
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\Biggl[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{\pi_{i}})-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(X_{\pi_{i+n}})\biggr)\geq t+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{2N\gamma}}\ \Bigg|\ X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}\Biggr]\leq\exp\biggl(-\frac{N\gamma^{2}t^{2}}{2\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}\biggr).

An exponential tail decay in the above comes at a price — it requires that
n
and
m
are well-balanced in the sense that
N\gamma^{2}
is large. Thus it can be used to show that
\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})=o_{P}(1)
under the limited regime where
N\gamma^{2}\rightarrow\infty
. The lemma below removes this unnecessary constraint at the expense of having a polynomial tail decay.

Lemma 11

(Markov for the Permuted MMD)
.

Under the setting of
Lemma

10
with
N=n+m
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Biggl[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{\pi_{i}})-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(X_{\pi_{i+n}})\biggr)\geq t\Biggr]\leq\frac{2K}{nt^{2}}\quad\text{for all $t>0$.}

Hence
\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m}^{\bm{\pi}})=o_{P}(1)
, provided that
Kn^{-1}\rightarrow 0
.

Proof.

Markov’s inequality together with the V-statistic representation of the empirical MMD yields that for any
t>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Biggl[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{\pi_{i}})-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(X_{\pi_{i+n}})\biggr)\geq t\Biggr]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t^{2}}\biggl\{\underbrace{\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[k(X_{\pi_{i}},X_{\pi_{j}})]}_{\mathrm{(I)}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}[k(X_{\pi_{i+n}},X_{\pi_{j+n}})]}_{\mathrm{(II)}}-\underbrace{\frac{2}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}[k(X_{\pi_{i}},X_{\pi_{j+n}})]}_{\mathrm{(III)}}\biggr\}.

Exploiting the uniformity of permutation
\bm{\pi}
, we analyze the terms (I), (II) and (III), separately. The analyses of the first two terms (I) and (II) are similar, and we note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[k(X_{\pi_{i}},X_{\pi_{i}})]=\mathbb{E}[k(X_{\pi_{i+n}},X_{\pi_{i+n}})]~=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{i},Y_{i})]+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}[k(Z_{j},Z_{j})]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{n}{N}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Y_{1})]+\frac{m}{N}\mathbb{E}[k(Z_{1},Z_{1})]

and for
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[k(X_{\pi_{i}},X_{\pi_{j}})]=\mathbb{E}[k(X_{\pi_{i+n}},X_{\pi_{j+n}})]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\biggl\{\sum_{(i,j)\in\bm{i}_{2}^{n}}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{i},Y_{j})]+\sum_{(i,j)\in\bm{i}_{2}^{m}}\mathbb{E}[k(Z_{i},Z_{j})]+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{i},Z_{j})]\bigg\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{n(n-1)}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Y_{2})]+\frac{m(m-1)}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Z_{1},Z_{2})]+\frac{2nm}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Z_{1})].

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathrm{(I)}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{n}{nN}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Y_{1})]+\frac{m}{nN}\mathbb{E}[k(Z_{1},Z_{1})]

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{n-1}{n}\biggl\{\frac{n(n-1)}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Y_{2})]+\frac{m(m-1)}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Z_{1},Z_{2})]+\frac{2nm}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Z_{1})]\biggr\}

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{(II)}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{n}{mN}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Y_{1})]+\frac{m}{mN}\mathbb{E}[k(Z_{1},Z_{1})]

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{m-1}{m}\biggl\{\frac{n(n-1)}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Y_{2})]+\frac{m(m-1)}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Z_{1},Z_{2})]+\frac{2nm}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Z_{1})]\biggr\}.

On the other hand, the last term (III) is

\displaystyle\mathrm{(III)}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{2n(n-1)}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Y_{2})]+\frac{2m(m-1)}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Z_{1},Z_{2})]+\frac{4nm}{N(N-1)}\mathbb{E}[k(Y_{1},Z_{1})].

Given that kernel
k
is non-negative and bounded by
K
, we can upper bound
\mathrm{(I)}+\mathrm{(II)}-\mathrm{(III)}
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{(I)}+\mathrm{(II)}-\mathrm{(III)}~\leq~

\displaystyle\biggl(\frac{n}{nN}+\frac{m}{nN}+\frac{n}{mN}+\frac{m}{mN}\biggr)K=\frac{n+m}{nm}K

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{2K}{n}.

Hence the result follows.
∎

The lemma below presents a concentration bound for the permuted HSIC statistic. It is worth noting that obtaining the logarithmic dependence on
\alpha
is non-trivial, and thus we highlight it as our contribution.

Lemma 12

(Concentration Inequality for Permuted HSIC)
.

Assume that the kernels
k
and
\ell
are bounded as
0\leq k(y,y^{\prime})\leq K
and
0\leq\ell(z,z^{\prime})\leq L
for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Y}
and
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\biggl(\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\geq C\sqrt{\frac{KL}{n}}\max\bigg\{\log^{1/4}\biggl(\frac{1}{\alpha}\biggr),\log^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{\alpha}\biggr),1\bigg\}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\biggr)\leq\alpha,

where
C
is some positive constant.

Proof.

The proof is based on
Kim et al., 2022a
that establishes an exponential tail bound for a permuted U-statistic of HSIC. Given
\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}}
, let us denote the permuted U-statistic by

\displaystyle U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{(n-2)!}{n!}\sum_{(i,j)\in\bm{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j}})+\frac{(n-4)!}{n!}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2})\in\bm{i}_{4}^{n}}k(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i_{2}}},Z_{\pi_{j_{2}}})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{2(n-3)!}{n!}\sum_{(i,j_{1},j_{2})\in\bm{i}_{3}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j_{2}}}),

where
\bm{i}_{m}^{n}
stands for the set of all
m
-tuples drawn from
[n]
without replacement.
Kim et al., 2022a
shows that
U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\bigl(U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}\geq t\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\bigr)\leq\exp\bigg\{\!-C_{1}\min\biggl(\frac{t^{2}}{\Sigma_{n}^{2}},\ \frac{t}{\Sigma_{n}}\biggr)\bigg\}\quad\text{for all $t>0$,}

where

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n^{2}(n-1)^{2}}\sup_{\bm{\pi}\in\bm{\Pi}_{n}}\Biggl\{\sum_{(i,j)\in\bm{i}_{2}^{n}}k^{2}(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell^{2}(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j}})\Bigg\}\leq\frac{1}{n(n-1)}K^{2}L^{2}.

The inequality above holds under the assumption that
k
and
\ell
are uniformly bounded by
K
and
L
. Therefore, under the assumption of
Lemma

12
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\bigl(U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}\geq t\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\bigr)\leq\exp\bigg\{\!-C_{2}\min\biggl(\frac{n^{2}t^{2}}{K^{2}L^{2}},\ \frac{nt}{KL}\biggr)\bigg\}\quad\text{for all $t>0$.}

(55)

On the other hand, the squared empirical HSIC can be written in the form of a V-statistic given as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})~=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j}})+\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{n}k(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i_{2}}},Z_{\pi_{j_{2}}})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n^{3}}\sum_{i,j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j_{2}}}),

and we note that the difference between the U-statistic and the V-statistic is bounded as

\displaystyle\big|U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}-\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\big|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{(n-2)!}{n!}\sum_{(i,j)\in\bm{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j}})-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j}})\bigg|

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{(n-4)!}{n!}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2})\in\bm{i}_{4}^{n}}k(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i_{2}}},Z_{\pi_{j_{2}}})-\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{n}k(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i_{2}}},Z_{\pi_{j_{2}}})\bigg|

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{2(n-3)!}{n!}\sum_{(i,j_{1},j_{2})\in\bm{i}_{3}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j_{2}}})-\frac{2}{n^{3}}\sum_{i,j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{\pi_{i}},Z_{\pi_{j_{2}}})\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C_{3}\frac{KL}{n},

(56)

for any
\bm{\pi}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}
. Using the above bound (
56
) and letting
t>0
, we convert the concentration inequality for the permuted empirical HSIC into that for the permuted U-statistic as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\geq t\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\bigr)~=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})\geq t^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\biggl(\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}+U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}\geq t^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\bigl(\big|\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}})-U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}\big|+U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}\geq t^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\biggl(U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}\geq t^{2}-C_{3}\frac{KL}{n}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\biggr)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\pi}}\biggl(U_{\bm{\pi},\mathrm{HSIC}}\geq\frac{t^{2}}{2}\,|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\biggr)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(iii)}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\exp\bigg\{\!-C_{4}\min\biggl(\frac{n^{2}t^{4}}{K^{2}L^{2}},\ \frac{nt^{2}}{KL}\biggr)\bigg\}

where step (i) uses the bound (
56
), step (ii) assumes that
t^{2}\geq 2C_{3}\frac{KL}{n}
and step (iii) follows by concentration inequality (
55
). Setting the last exponential bound to
\alpha
and solving for
t
yield the desired result.
∎

We recall the concentration inequality of the empirical MMD presented by
Gretton et al., 2012
, which has been used in various places throughout the paper.

Lemma 13

(Theorem 7 of
Gretton et al., 2012
)
.

Assume that the kernel
k
is bounded as
0\leq k(x,y)\leq K
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{S}
. Then for any
t>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl\{\bigg|\widehat{\mathrm{MMD}}(\mathcal{X}_{n+m})-\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)\bigg|>2\biggl(\sqrt{\frac{K}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{K}{n}}\biggr)+t\biggr\}\leq 2\exp\biggl(-\frac{t^{2}mn}{2K(m+n)}\biggr).

The following is a counterpart to
Lemma

13
for the empirical HSIC. While similar results exist in the literature
(e.g.,
Gretton et al., 2005
, Theorem 3)
, none precisely align with our specific requirements. Hence we opt to present a detailed proof of the following result.

Lemma 14

(Exponential Inequality for the Empirical HSIC)
.

Assume that the kernels
k
and
\ell
are bounded as
0\leq k(y,y^{\prime})\leq K
and
0\leq\ell(z,z^{\prime})\leq L
for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Y}
and
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then for any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl\{\big|\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n})-\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ})\big|\geq C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{KL}{n}}+t\bigg\}\leq 2\exp\biggl(-\frac{C_{2}t^{2}n}{KL}\biggr),

where
C_{1},C_{2}
are positive constants.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we denote by
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
some generic positive constants. By
Lemma

6
, the empirical HSIC has global sensitivity at most
C_{1}\sqrt{KL}/n
. Thus an application of McDiarmid’s inequality
(
Gretton et al., 2012
, Theorem 29)
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl\{\big|\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n})-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n})\bigr]\big|\geq t\big\}\leq 2\exp\biggl(-\frac{C_{2}t^{2}n}{KL}\biggr)\quad\text{for all $t\geq 0$.}

(57)

Now we bound the difference between the expectation of
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n})
and the population HSIC as

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}\bigl[\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n})\bigr]-\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ})\big|

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{i})-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{j})\bigg\}\biggr]-\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\big\{\mathbb{E}_{P_{YZ}}[f(Y,Z)]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{Y}P_{Z}}[f(Y,Z)]\big\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{i})-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{j})\bigg\}-\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\big\{\mathbb{E}_{P_{YZ}}[f(Y,Z)]-\mathbb{E}_{P_{Y}P_{Z}}[f(Y,Z)]\big\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{YZ}}[f(Y,Z)]-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{j})+\mathbb{E}_{P_{Y}P_{Z}}[f(Y,Z)]\bigg\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(iii)}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{YZ}}[f(Y,Z)]\bigg|}_{\coloneqq\mathrm{(I)}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{j})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{Y}P_{Z}}[f(Y,Z)]\bigg|}_{\coloneqq\mathrm{(II)}},

where step (i) uses Jensen’s inequality, step (ii) uses the reverse triangle inequality and step (iii) follows by the triangle inequality. Let
\{(\tilde{Y}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
be i.i.d. copies of
(Y_{1},Z_{1})
, and
\{\epsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be i.i.d. Rademacher random variables. Then Jensen’s inequality in conjunction with symmetrization yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{(I)}~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\big\{f(Y_{i},Z_{i})-f(\tilde{Y}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{i})\big\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(Y_{i},Z_{i})\bigg|~\leq~2\sqrt{\frac{KL}{n}},

(58)

where the last inequality is due to
Bartlett and Mendelson, 2002
. For the second term,

\displaystyle\mathrm{(II)}~\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{j})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{Y}P_{Z}}[f(Y,Z)]\bigg|

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=2}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{1})+\frac{f(Y_{1},Z_{1})}{n}-\mathbb{E}_{P_{Y}P_{Z}}[f(Y,Z)]\bigg|

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{ii})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=2}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{1})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{Y}P_{Z}}[f(Y,Z)]\bigg|+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}|f(Y_{1},Z_{1})|

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{iii})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=2}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{1})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{Y}P_{Z}}[f(Y,Z)]\bigg|

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}|f(Y_{i},Z_{1})|+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}|f(Y_{1},Z_{1})|,

where step (i) follows by Jensen’s inequality and step (ii) and step (iii) use the triangle inequality. Let
\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}
be the reproducing kernel Hilbert space with the product kernel
k\otimes\ell
. Then by the reproducing property and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}|f(y,z)|=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}|\langle k(\cdot,y)\ell(\cdot,z),f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}|\leq\sqrt{k(y,y)\ell(z,z)}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k\otimes\ell}}\leq\sqrt{KL}

for all
y\in\mathbb{Y},z\in\mathbb{Z}
. Using this inequality and letting
\{(\tilde{Y}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{1})\}_{i=2}^{n}
be i.i.d. copies of
\{(Y_{i},Z_{1})\}_{i=2}^{n}
and recalling that
\{\epsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are i.i.d. Rademacher random variables, the second term can be further bounded by

\displaystyle\mathrm{(II)}~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=2}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{1})-\mathbb{E}_{P_{Y}P_{Z}}[f(Y,Z)]\bigg|+\frac{2\sqrt{KL}}{n}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=2}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{1})-\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}[f(\tilde{Y}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{1})]\bigg|+\frac{2\sqrt{KL}}{n}

(59)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=2}^{n}f(Y_{i},Z_{1})-f(\tilde{Y}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{1})\bigg|+\frac{2\sqrt{KL}}{n}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\{f(Y_{i},Z_{1})-f(\tilde{Y}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{1})\}\bigg|\,\Big|\,Z_{1},\tilde{Z}_{1}\biggr]\biggr\}+\frac{2\sqrt{KL}}{n}

(60)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{k\otimes\ell}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(Y_{i},Z_{1})\bigg|\,\Big|\,Z_{1}\biggr]\biggr\}+\frac{2\sqrt{KL}}{n}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\frac{KL}{n-1}}+\frac{2\sqrt{KL}}{n},

(61)

where the last inequality is due to
Bartlett and Mendelson, 2002
. Putting the inequalities (
58
) and (
61
) together, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}\bigl[\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n})\bigr]-\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ})\big|\leq C_{3}\sqrt{\frac{KL}{n}}.

The above inequality along with McDiarmid’s inequality (
57
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl\{\big|\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}(\mathcal{X}_{n})-\mathrm{HSIC}_{k\otimes\ell}(P_{YZ})\big|\geq t+C_{3}\sqrt{\frac{KL}{n}}\bigg\}\leq 2\exp\biggl(-\frac{C_{2}t^{2}n}{KL}\biggr)\quad\text{for all $t\geq 0$.}

Hence we complete the proof of
Lemma

14
.
∎

The below lemma is folklore in the literature
(
e.g.
,
Romano and Wolf, 2005
, Lemma 1)
where we recall
\lfloor x\rfloor
denotes the largest integer smaller than or equal to
x
. We provide a proof for completeness.

Lemma 15

(Permutation
p
-value)
.

Suppose that
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},X_{n+1}
are exchangeable random variables. Then for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{1}{n+1}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}\leq X_{i})+1\bigg\}\leq\alpha\biggr)\leq\frac{\lfloor(n+1)\alpha\rfloor}{n+1}\leq\alpha.

Suppose further that
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}
are all distinct with probability one. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{1}{n+1}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}\leq X_{i})+1\bigg\}\leq\alpha\biggr)=\frac{\lfloor(n+1)\alpha\rfloor}{n+1}.

Proof.

Let
X_{(1)}\leq X_{(2)}\leq\ldots\leq X_{(n+1)}
be the order statistics of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}
. Then we have a series of the identities:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}\leq X_{i})+1\bigg\}=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}\leq X_{i})\leq\alpha

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}\leq X_{i})\leq\lfloor(n+1)\alpha\rfloor

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}<X_{n+1})\geq(n+1)-\lfloor(n+1)\alpha\rfloor\coloneqq k

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow~

\displaystyle X_{n+1}>X_{(k)}.

Now by the exchangeability condition, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{n+1}>X_{(k)})~=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>X_{(k)})\bigg].

On the other hand, by the definition of
X_{(k)}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>X_{(k)})\leq\frac{n+1-k}{n+1}=\frac{\lfloor(n+1)\alpha\rfloor}{n+1}.

Hence the first result follows. When
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}
are all distinct, observe

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>X_{(k)})=n+1-k.

Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>X_{(k)})\bigg]=\frac{n+1-k}{n+1}=\frac{\lfloor(n+1)\alpha\rfloor}{n+1}.

∎

The permutation test is often slightly conservative due to its discrete nature. The following randomization trick is well-known in the literature
(e.g.,
Lehmann and Romano, 2005
, Chapter 15)
, which modifies the permutation test to have type I error exactly equal to
\alpha
.

Lemma 16

(Randomized Tests)
.

For given
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\gamma\in(0,\alpha]
, consider a test function
\phi
such that
\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}(\phi=1)=\gamma\leq\alpha
. Letting
U
be a uniform random variable on
[0,1]
independent of
\phi
, define a randomized test
\phi_{\mathrm{rand}}
as

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathrm{rand}}=\phi+(1-\phi)\times\mathds{1}\biggl(U\leq\frac{\alpha-\gamma}{1-\gamma}\biggr).

The randomized test
\phi_{\mathrm{rand}}
has type I error exactly equal to
\alpha
, and power never worse than that of
\phi
,
i.e.
,
\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}(\phi_{\mathrm{rand}}=1)=\alpha
and
\mathbb{P}_{H_{1}}(\phi_{\mathrm{rand}}=1)\geq\mathbb{P}_{H_{1}}(\phi=1)
.

Proof.

Type I error control is immediate given that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{H_{0}}[\phi_{\mathrm{rand}}]~=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{H_{0}}[\phi]+\mathbb{E}_{H_{0}}[1-\phi]\times\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\biggl(U\leq\frac{\alpha-\gamma}{1-\gamma}\biggr)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\gamma+(1-\gamma)\times\frac{\alpha-\gamma}{1-\gamma}=\alpha.

The second claim about the power is also immediate given that
\phi_{\mathrm{rand}}\geq\phi
.
∎

The permutation test can be expressed in terms of the permutation
p
-value as well as the quantile of the permutation distribution, which can be justified by the following lemma.

Lemma 17

(Quantile Representation)
.

For any
\alpha\in[0,1]
and dataset
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}\}
, we have the identity

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\biggl(\frac{1}{n+1}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}\leq X_{i})+1\bigg\}\leq\alpha\biggr)=\mathds{1}\bigl(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha}\bigr),

where
q_{1-\alpha}
is the
1-\alpha
quantile of
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}\}
given as

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha}=

\displaystyle\inf\biggl\{t\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\leq t)\geq 1-\alpha\biggr\}.

Moreover, by letting
X_{(\lceil(1-\alpha)(n+1)\rceil)}
denote the
\lceil(1-\alpha)(n+1)\rceil
th order statistic of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}
and
X_{(0)}=-\infty
, we have
q_{1-\alpha}=X_{(\lceil(1-\alpha)(n+1)\rceil)}
.

Proof.

The second claim follows by noting that

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha}~=~

\displaystyle\inf\biggl\{t\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\leq t)\geq 1-\alpha\biggr\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\inf\biggl\{t\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}<t)\geq 1-\alpha\biggr\}

(62)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\inf\biggl\{t\in\mathbb{R}:\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}<t)\geq(1-\alpha)(n+1)\biggr\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle X_{(\lceil(1-\alpha)(n+1)\rceil)}.

For the first claim, denote
G(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}<x)
, which is a left-continuous step function. We then have

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\biggl(\frac{1}{n+1}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}\leq X_{i})+1\bigg\}\leq\alpha\biggr)~=~

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\bigl(G(X_{n+1})\geq(1-\alpha)(n+1)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})

where the last step follows since
G
is a left-continuous step function. In more detail, suppose that
G(X_{n+1})\geq(1-\alpha)(n+1)
. Since
G
is a left-continuous step function, there exists a small constant
\epsilon>0
such that
G(X_{n+1}-\epsilon)=G(X_{n+1})\geq(1-\alpha)(n+1)
. Therefore,
X_{n+1}
cannot be the
1-\alpha
quantile and should be greater than
q_{1-\alpha}
.

Moreover, the event
X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha}
implies that
G(X_{n+1})\geq(1-\alpha)(n+1)
by the definition of
q_{1-\alpha}
as in expression (
62
). Hence we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\bigl(G(X_{n+1})\geq(1-\alpha)(n+1)\bigr)~=~\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha}),

and the first claim follows.
∎

The next lemma plays a crucial role in proving the validity of the differentially private permutation test in
Algorithm

1
.

Lemma 18

(Alternative Expression)
.

Given
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
n\geq 1
, set

\displaystyle\alpha_{\star}=\max\bigg\{\biggl(\frac{n+1}{n}\alpha-\frac{1}{n}\biggr),\,0\bigg\}.

Then for dataset
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}\}
, we have the identity

\displaystyle\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})=\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>r_{1-\alpha_{\star}})\mathds{1}\biggl(\alpha\geq\frac{1}{n+1}\biggr),

where
q_{1-\alpha}
and
r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}
are the
1-\alpha
quantile of
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n+1}
and the
1-\alpha_{\star}
quantile of
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, respectively.

Proof.

For
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{n+1})
,
q_{1-\alpha}
becomes the maximum of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}
for which
\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})=0
. Similarly, it becomes

\displaystyle\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>r_{1-\alpha_{\star}})\mathds{1}\biggl(\alpha\geq\frac{1}{n+1}\biggr)=0.

Hence we only need to verify the identity under
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{n+1}
. In what follows, we assume
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{n+1}
and show that
\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})=\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>r_{1-\alpha_{\star}})
.

Remark that the
1-\alpha
quantile of
\{X_{1}+c,\ldots,X_{n+1}+c\}
is the same as the
1-\alpha
quantile of
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}\}
plus
c
for any
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Using this location-shift property of quantiles, observe that

\displaystyle\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})~=~

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\biggl(0>\inf\biggl\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}-X_{n+1}\leq x)\geq 1-\alpha\biggr\}\biggr)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\biggl(0>\inf\biggl\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}-X_{n+1}\leq x)\geq\frac{n+1}{n}(1-\alpha)-\frac{\mathds{1}(0\leq x)}{n}\biggr\}\biggr)

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\biggl(0>\inf\biggl\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}-X_{n+1}\leq x)\geq\frac{n+1}{n}(1-\alpha)\biggr\}\biggr)

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}~

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\biggl(0>\inf\biggl\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}-X_{n+1}\leq x)\geq\min\biggl[\frac{n+1}{n}(1-\alpha),\,1\biggr]\biggr\}\biggr)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}).

where the equality (
\dagger
) holds under
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{n+1}
. Therefore it holds that
\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})\geq\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>r_{1-\alpha_{\star}})
.

Next we prove the other direction
\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})\leq\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>r_{1-\alpha_{\star}})
. Note that the infimum in the definition of a quantile can be replaced by the minimum so that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathds{1}(X_{i}-X_{n+1}\leq\underbrace{q_{1-\alpha}-X_{n+1}}_{\coloneqq\tilde{q}_{1-\alpha}})\geq 1-\alpha.

Having this in mind, for
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{n+1}
, assume
X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha}
(equivalently
0>\tilde{q}_{1-\alpha}
) under which it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}-X_{n+1}\leq\tilde{q}_{1-\alpha})\geq\frac{n+1}{n}(1-\alpha)-\frac{\mathds{1}(0\leq\tilde{q}_{1-\alpha})}{n}=\frac{n+1}{n}(1-\alpha)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}-X_{n+1}\leq\tilde{q}_{1-\alpha})\geq\min\biggl\{\frac{n+1}{n}(1-\alpha),\,1\biggr\}.

This implies that
\tilde{q}_{1-\alpha}\geq r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}-X_{n+1}\coloneqq\tilde{r}_{1-\alpha_{\star}}
by the definition of
r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}
. Consequently,
\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})\leq\mathds{1}(\tilde{q}_{1-\alpha}\geq\tilde{r}_{1-\alpha_{\star}})
, which further implies that

\displaystyle\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})\leq\mathds{1}(\tilde{q}_{1-\alpha}\geq\tilde{r}_{1-\alpha_{\star}})\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})\leq\mathds{1}(0>\tilde{r}_{1-\alpha_{\star}})=\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>r_{1-\alpha_{\star}}).

Thus we conclude that
\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>q_{1-\alpha})=\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}>r_{1-\alpha_{\star}})
for
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{n+1}
as well. This completes the proof of
Lemma

18
.
∎

The next result is concerned with the global sensitivity of the quantiles.

Lemma 19

(Sensitivity of Quantiles)
.

Suppose that the test statistic
T
has the global sensitivity at most
\Delta_{T}
as in (
4
). Let us denote by

r_{1-\alpha}\bigl(\mathcal{X}_{n};\{\bm{\pi}_{i},\zeta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}\bigr)
the
1-\alpha
quantile of
\{M_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}
where
