M_{i}=T(\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}_{i}})+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{i}
with
\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}=\varepsilon+\log(1/(1-\delta))
and
\zeta_{i}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathsf{Laplace}(0,1)
for
i\in[B]
. Then for any
\alpha\in[0,1)
, the global sensitivity of the
1-\alpha
quantile satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}:\\
d_{\mathrm{ham}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n})\leq 1\end{subarray}}\big|r_{1-\alpha}\bigl(\mathcal{X}_{n};\{\bm{\pi}_{i},\zeta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}\bigr)-r_{1-\alpha}\bigl(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n};\{\bm{\pi}_{i},\zeta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}\bigr)\big|\leq\Delta_{T},

for any permutations
\bm{\pi}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\pi}_{B}
and any
\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathsf{Laplace}(0,1)
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
be a neighboring dataset of
\mathcal{X}_{n}
where
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}
differ only in their
k
th component for some
k\in[n]
. Denote the permuted test statistics computed on
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}_{1}},\ldots,\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}^{\bm{\pi}_{B}}
by
\tilde{T}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{T}_{B}
. For simplicity, we write
r_{1-\alpha}=r_{1-\alpha}\bigl(\mathcal{X}_{n};\{\bm{\pi}_{i},\zeta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}\bigr)
and
\tilde{r}_{1-\alpha}=r_{1-\alpha}\bigl(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n};\{\bm{\pi}_{i},\zeta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}\bigr)
. Having this notation, first note that
T_{i}\geq\tilde{T}_{i}-\Delta_{T}
for all
i\in[B]
under the assumption of (
4
) and thus

\displaystyle{1-\alpha}\leq\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(T_{i}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{i}\leq r_{1-\alpha})\leq\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(\tilde{T}_{i}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{i}\leq r_{1-\alpha}+\Delta_{T}),

which implies that
\tilde{r}_{1-\alpha}\leq r_{1-\alpha}+\Delta_{T}
.

Next we argue that
\tilde{r}_{1-\alpha}\geq r_{1-\alpha}-\Delta_{T}
. For this direction, let
\epsilon>0
be an arbitrary constant. Then by the definition of
r_{1-\alpha}
and
T_{i}\leq\tilde{T}_{i}+\Delta_{T}
for all
i\in[B]
,

\displaystyle{1-\alpha}>\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(T_{i}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{i}\leq r_{1-\alpha}-\epsilon)\geq\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(\tilde{T}_{i}+2\Delta_{T}\xi_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}\zeta_{i}\leq r_{1-\alpha}-\epsilon-\Delta_{T}).

Hence
\tilde{r}_{1-\alpha}>r_{1-\alpha}-\epsilon-\Delta_{T}
. Since
\epsilon
is arbitrary, we conclude
\tilde{r}_{1-\alpha}\geq r_{1-\alpha}-\Delta_{T}
. In summary, we have established that
|r_{1-\alpha}-\tilde{r}_{1-\alpha}|\leq\Delta_{T}
, which holds for any
k\in[n]
, any permutations
\bm{\pi}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\pi}_{B}
and any
\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{B}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathsf{Laplace}(0,1)
. Therefore, the desired claim follows.
∎

The Laplace mechanism introduces a perturbed statistic by adding Laplace noise. Instead of directly studying quantiles of this perturbed statistic, it is often easier to analyze the quantiles of the original statistic and the quantiles of the added Laplace noise separately. These results can then be combined using the following quantile inequality.

Lemma 20

(Quantile Inequality)
.

Let
q_{1-\alpha}^{X+Y}
be the
1-\alpha
quantile of
X+Y
. Similarly, let
q_{1-\alpha/2}^{X}
and
q_{1-\alpha/2}^{Y}
be the
1-\alpha/2
quantile of
X
and
Y
, respectively. Then
q_{1-\alpha}^{X+Y}\leq q_{1-\alpha/2}^{X}+q_{1-\alpha/2}^{Y}
.

Proof.

Note that if
\{X+Y>q_{1-\alpha/2}^{X}+q_{1-\alpha/2}^{Y}\}
, then at least one of the events
\{X>q_{1-\alpha/2}^{X}\}
and
\{Y>q_{1-\alpha/2}^{Y}\}
should occur (otherwise contradiction). This together with the union bound yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(X+Y>q_{1-\alpha/2}^{X}+q_{1-\alpha/2}^{Y}\bigr)~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(X>q_{1-\alpha/2}^{X}\bigr)+\mathbb{P}\bigl(Y>q_{1-\alpha/2}^{Y}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\alpha,

where the last inequality follows by the definition of quantile. The above implies that the
1-\alpha
quantile of
X+Y
is less than or equal to
q_{1-\alpha/2}^{X}+q_{1-\alpha/2}^{Y}
and therefore the claim follows.
∎

The following lemma provides a high-probability upper bound for the quantile of an empirical distribution in relation to a theoretical distribution. In the context of the permutation test, this result enables us to eliminate the randomness from Monte Carlo simulations during the power analysis.

Lemma 21

(Quantile Approximation)
.

Let
\{X,X_{1},\ldots,X_{B}\}
be i.i.d. random variables conditional on a sigma field
\mathcal{G}
, and let
X_{0}
be another random variable defined on the same probability space as
X
. For
\alpha\in(0,1)
, define
\widehat{q}_{1-\alpha}
and
q_{1-\alpha}
as

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{1-\alpha}:=\inf\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{B+1}\sum_{i=0}^{B}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\leq x)\geq 1-\alpha\bigg\}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha}:=\inf\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathbb{P}(X\leq x\,|\,\mathcal{G})\geq 1-\alpha\bigg\}.

Assume that the conditional distribution of
X
given
\mathcal{G}
has no atom. Then for any
\beta\in(0,1)
,
\delta>0
,
\gamma\in\bigl(0,\alpha/(1+\delta)\bigr)
, and
B
satisfying

\displaystyle B\geq\max\bigg\{\frac{2+\delta}{\delta^{2}\gamma}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\beta}\biggr),\ \frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha-\gamma(1+\delta)}\bigg\},

it holds that
\widehat{q}_{1-\alpha}\leq q_{1-\gamma}
with probability at least
1-\beta
.

Remark 4
.

•

For simplicity, if we set
\gamma=\alpha/6
and
\delta=2
, then
Lemma

21
ensures
\widehat{q}_{1-\alpha}\leq q_{1-\alpha/6}
with probability at least
1-\beta
provided that

\displaystyle B\geq 2\alpha^{-1}\max\big\{3\log(1/\beta),\,1-\alpha\big\},

(63)

or more simply
B\geq 6\alpha^{-1}\log(1/\beta)
if
\beta<e^{-1/3}\approx 0.716
. The constant factors in the above condition can be further improved by choosing a smaller value of
\gamma
and a larger value of
\delta
. This improvement, on the other hand, comes at the cost of inflating the constant factor in the minimum separation of a test as we are ultimately concerned with a smaller value of the significance level.

•

We also remark that this condition offers an improvement over the previous requirements for
B
imposed, such as, in
Kim et al., 2022a
;
Schrab et al., 2023
. It particularly improves the dependence of
\alpha
from
\alpha^{-2}
to
\alpha^{-1}
. The previous approach relies on the additive form of concentration inequalities such as the Dvoretzky–Kiefer–Wolfowitz (DKW) inequality
(
Massart, 1990
)
. On the other hand, we use a multiplicative Chernoff bound, which is the key to this improvement. Another technique to obtain the
\alpha^{-1}
factor in the number of
B
is

•

Domingo-Enrich et al., 2023
derive a high-probability upper bound for
B
using the property of order statistics. This technique, completely different from ours, also yields a factor of
\alpha^{-1}
for the number of
B
. In particular, their condition requires that
B\geq\alpha^{-1}-1
, and moreover, the coreset count in their Theorem 1 must be larger than some function of
B,\alpha,\beta
.

•

Lemma

21
assumes that the conditional distribution of
W
given
\mathcal{G}
has no atom. In our context, this assumption is easily met as we add continuous noise to a test statistic through the Laplace mechanism. In a more general context, such a guarantee can be achieved, for example, through jittering.

Proof.

As mentioned in
Remark

4
, a main tool for
Lemma

21
is a multiplicative Chernoff bound
3
3

3

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernoff_bound
, which we recall here:
Suppose that
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
are independent random variables taking values in
\{0,1\}
. Let
Y=\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}
denote their sum and let
\mu=\mathbb{E}[Y]
denote the expected value of the sum. Then for any
\delta\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(Y\geq(1+\delta)\mu)\bigr)\leq e^{-\delta^{2}\mu/(2+\delta)}.

(64)

Given this multiplicative bound, note that
\widehat{q}_{1-\alpha}
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{1-\alpha}=\inf\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{B+1}\sum_{i=0}^{B}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>x)\leq\alpha\bigg\}.

For a fixed
x
(which will be specified later on) and
\delta>0
, observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B+1}\sum_{i=0}^{B}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>x)~\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B+1}+\frac{1}{B+1}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>x)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B+1}+\frac{B(1+\delta)}{B+1}\biggl[\frac{1}{1+\delta}\times\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>x)\biggr].

(65)

Note that the multiplicative Chernoff (
64
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>x)\geq(1+\delta)\mathbb{P}(X>x\,|\,\mathcal{G})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{G}\biggr)\leq\exp\biggl(-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2+\delta}B\mathbb{P}(X>x\,|\,\mathcal{G})\biggr)\overset{\text{set}}{\leq}\beta.

Therefore with probability at least
1-\beta
, the following event holds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>x)<(1+\delta)\mathbb{P}(X>x\,|\,\mathcal{G})~\Longleftrightarrow~\frac{1}{1+\delta}\times\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>x)<\mathbb{P}(X>x\,|\,\mathcal{G}).

In particular, this condition holds when

\displaystyle B\geq\frac{2+\delta}{\delta^{2}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}(X>x\,|\,\mathcal{G})}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\beta}\biggr).

(66)

Now we take
x=q_{1-\gamma}
, which gives that
\mathbb{P}(X>x\,|\,\mathcal{G})=\gamma
since the conditional distribution of
X
given
\mathcal{G}
has no atom. Then, continuing from inequality (
65
), we have under the event stated above that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B+1}\sum_{i=0}^{B}\mathds{1}(X_{i}>q_{1-\gamma})~\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B+1}+\frac{B(1+\delta)}{B+1}\mathbb{P}(X>q_{1-\gamma}\,|\,\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B+1}+\frac{B(1+\delta)}{B+1}\gamma.

Now assume that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B+1}+\frac{B(1+\delta)}{B+1}\gamma\leq\alpha.

(67)

Then with probability at least
1-\beta
, the definition of
\widehat{q}_{1-\alpha}
yields

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{1-\alpha}\leq q_{1-\gamma}.

There are two conditions that ensures the above inequality, namely condition (
66
) and condition (
67
). Under the condition that
\alpha>\gamma(1+\delta)
, condition (
67
) becomes equivalent to

\displaystyle B\geq\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha-\gamma(1+\delta)}.

Hence, a sufficient condition for
B
that ensures
\widehat{q}_{1-\alpha}\leq q_{1-\gamma}
is given as

\displaystyle B\geq\max\bigg\{\frac{2+\delta}{\delta^{2}\gamma}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\beta}\biggr),\ \frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha-\gamma(1+\delta)}\bigg\}.

This completes the proof of
Lemma

21
.
∎

The following lemma computes the difference between the V-statistic and U-statistic of the squared HSIC, which is useful in the proof of
Theorem

14
.

Lemma 22

(Difference between
V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
and
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
)
.

Recall
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}
given in (
12
) and
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
given in (
21
). Suppose that kernels satisfy
k(y,y)=K
and
\ell(z,z)=L
for all
y\in\mathbb{Y}
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then the difference between
\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}
and
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
is computed as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{HSIC}}^{2}-U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}=D_{1}+D_{2},

where

\displaystyle D_{1}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{n-1}{n^{2}}KL-\frac{L}{n^{3}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})-\frac{K}{n^{3}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j})

\displaystyle D_{2}~=~

\displaystyle-\frac{3n^{2}-4n+2}{(n-1)n^{4}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j})+\frac{2(5n^{2}-8n+4)}{n^{4}(n-1)(n-2)}\sum_{(i,j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{3}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j_{2}})

\displaystyle-\frac{6n^{2}-11n+6}{n^{4}(n-1)(n-2)(n-3)}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{4}^{n}}k(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})\ell(Z_{j_{1}},Z_{j_{2}}).

Remark
.

The term
D_{1}
is invariant to any permutation of either
Y
values or
Z
values.

Proof.

Write the squared empirical HSIC in (
12
) as
V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
and the U-statistic of the squared HSIC as
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
. Note that
V_{\mathrm{HSIC}}=V_{a}+V_{b}-2V_{c}
where

\displaystyle V_{a}~=~\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j}),\quad V_{b}~=~\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{n}k(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})\ell(Z_{j_{1}},Z_{j_{2}}),

\displaystyle V_{c}~=~\frac{1}{n^{3}}\sum_{i,j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{n}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j_{2}}),

and each term can be expressed as

\displaystyle V_{a}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{KL}{n}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j}),

\displaystyle V_{b}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{KL}{n^{3}}+\frac{2K}{n^{4}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j})+\frac{2L}{n^{4}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})+\frac{KLn(n-1)}{n^{4}}

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{K(n-2)}{n^{4}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j})+\frac{L(n-2)}{n^{4}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n^{4}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j})+\frac{4}{n^{4}}\sum_{(i,j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{3}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j_{2}})

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{3}^{n}}k(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}})\ell(Z_{j_{1}},Z_{j_{2}})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle V_{c}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{KL}{n^{2}}+\frac{K}{n^{3}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j})+\frac{L}{n^{3}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{3}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j})+\frac{1}{n^{3}}\sum_{(i,j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{3}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j_{2}}).

Note also that
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}
can be written as
U_{\mathrm{HSIC}}=U_{a}+U_{b}-2U_{c}
where

\displaystyle U_{a}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j}),

\displaystyle U_{b}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{(n-4)!}{n!}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{4}^{n}}k(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{i_{2}},Z_{j_{2}})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle U_{c}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n(n-1)(n-2)}\sum_{(i,j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{3}^{n}}k(Y_{i},Y_{j_{1}})\ell(Z_{i},Z_{j_{2}}).

The result follows by calculating
V_{a}-U_{a}
,
V_{b}-U_{b}
and
-2V_{c}+2U_{c}
, and simplifying their sum via algebra.
∎
